Aber es handelt sich dann um eine **ideale*  *Immanenz**, die uns auf weitere, wesensmäßig zugehörige Zusammenhänge möglicher Synthesen verweist. Jede Evidenz *stiftet* für mich eine bleibende Habe. Auf die selbst erschaute Wirklichkeit kann ich *immer wieder* zurückkommen, in Ketten neuer Evidenzen als *Restitutionen* der ersten Evidenz; so z.B. bei der Evidenz immanenter Gegebenheiten etwa in Form einer Kette anschaulicher Wiedererinnerungen mit der offenen Endlosigkeit, die, als potentiellen Horizont, das *Ich kann immer wieder* schafft.
>
> Ohne dergleichen Möglichkeiten wäre für uns kein stehendes und bleibendes Sein, keine reale und ideale Welt. Eine jede ist für uns aus der Evidenz bzw. der Präsumption, evident-machen und gewonnene Evidenz wiederholen zu können.
>
> Schon daraus geht hervor, daß die einzelne Evidenz für uns noch kein bleibendes Sein schafft. Jedes Seiende ist in einem weitesten Sinne *,,an sich"* und hat sich gegenüber das zufällige Für-mich der einzelnen Akte, und ebenso ist jede Wahrheit in diesem weitesten Sinne *,,Wahrheit an sich"*. Dieser weiteste Sinn des An-sich verweist also auf Evidenz, aber nicht auf eine Evidenz als Erlebnisstatsache, sondern auf gewisse im transzendentalen Ich und seinem Leben begründete Potentialitäten, zunächst auf diejenige *der* Unendlichkeit auf ein und dasselbe synthetisch bezogener Meinungen überhaupt, dann aber auch diejenige ihrer Bewährung, also auf potentielle, als Erlebnisstatsachen ins Unendliche wiederholbare Evidenzen.

*Note on the text.* The three paragraphs are the whole of §27, and they run across the page break at Hua I 95/96 without loss. The file marks "ideale Immanenz" with mixed bold and italic markup (`**ideale*  *Immanenz**`). Emphasis is certain; whether the edition has spaced type, italic or both cannot be told from the file. The italic "*der*" in "diejenige *der* Unendlichkeit" is not Husserl's emphasis. The apparatus (note to 96,12f.) gives "diejenige Unendlichkeit", without the article, for both the manuscript and Typescript C, so the article is the editor's and the italics seem to mark it as such; the translation keeps the italic only to show what the file has, and the reading does not argue from it. The commentary's block starts at "Every evidence establishes" and ends at "grounded in the transcendental ego and its life". It leaves out the opening sentences on ideal immanence and the closing specification of the two potentialities.

The section opens by conceding the difficulty §26 left. §26 ended by claiming that "every conceivable adequation arises as our verification, is our synthesis" (Hua I 95). But a synthesis is an experience, and experiences pass. "To be sure" (*Freilich*) admits that three identities are not "really inherent" (*reell*) moments of the experience "as it streams away" (*verströmenden*): the identity of the meant object, the identity of the truly existing object, and the identity of the agreement between them. The participle names the problem. What streams away cannot contain what stays. The answer is a phrase of two words, both emphasized: "ideal immanence" (*ideale Immanenz*). The identity belongs to consciousness, since there is nowhere else for it to be, but it is not a component of any experience. §11 had defined the world's transcendence as "irreal containedness" (*irreelles Beschlossensein*, Hua I 65). §27 names the same structure from the inside. What is irreal as a component is immanent as an ideal unity, and it "refers us" onward, to "further … connections of possible syntheses". Ideal immanence is not a place where the identity is stored. It is a pointer to syntheses not yet carried out.

The next sentences give the pointer its form, and the vocabulary is legal and economic. Every evidence "institutes" (*stiftet*, italic) "a lasting possession" (*bleibende Habe*). An act that passes founds something that stays, as a deed of foundation outlasts the signing. Later evidences are "restitutions" (*Restitutionen*, italic) of the first: they restore a possession to its owner, not a new acquisition. The engine is a nominalized clause, "the *I can always again*" (*das* Ich kann immer wieder), which is the grammatical subject that "creates" (*schafft*) the open endlessness as a potential horizon. The infinity is not a series laid out in advance. It is the reach of a capacity, and the capacity belongs to the ego. The counterfactual draws the consequence for the world: "Without such possibilities there would be for us no standing and abiding being". The world exists for us "from the evidence, or the presumption" of being able to repeat evidence, so the capacity itself is sometimes only presumed. That small "or" (*bzw.*) prepares §28, where for the external world presumption is all there is.

"Already from this it follows" then states the thesis, and the quotation marks carry it. "In itself" (*„an sich“*) and "truth in itself" are italicized and quoted at once. The italics claim the term and the quotation marks hold it at a distance. Husserl uses the traditional words and marks that they no longer mean what they meant. "In a widest sense", said twice, gives the new meaning by its opposite. What exists has over against it "the contingent for-me of the single acts" (*das zufällige Für-mich der einzelnen Akte*). The in-itself is opposed to the contingent for-me of single acts, not to the for-me as such. It remains a for-me, but a for-me of potentialities rather than of acts. The final sentence splits evidence in two: evidence "as a fact of experience" (*Erlebnisstatsache*) and evidence as potentiality. The in-itself refers to the second. The first potentiality is the infinity of all the meanings related to "one and the same", the second the infinity of their verifications. The last phrase brings the facts back, as "potential evidences, repeatable into infinity as facts of experience". No single fact makes the in-itself, but the in-itself is nothing beyond the infinity of possible facts.

The passage sits between §26's claim that truth is drawn only from evidence and §28's world as an infinite idea (row 18). §26 made truth our synthesis and so seemed to make it as fleeting as our acts. §27 secures abiding being without leaving subjectivity, and its examples are deliberately the easy ones, immanent givens recalled again and again. §28 then extends the structure to outer experience, where the "I can" meets one-sidedness and the possibility of the stroke that §23 called crossed-out being.

The habitual side of the passage becomes the theme of the Fourth Meditation. In §32 the act that decides "passes, but from now on I am, and abidingly, the ego decided thus and so" (Hua I 101). The "lasting possession" of §27 there becomes a property of the ego itself. The temporal side returns in §55 in almost the same words. My own past experience gains the sense of an existing one because "the original is gone, but in repeated presentifications I come back to it, and that in the evidence: *so I can always again*" (*so kann ich immer wieder*, Hua I 155). The identical experience is immanent to the separate recollections "only as something irreal" (*nur als Irreelles immanent*), §27's ideal immanence restated. §55 then applies the result to ideal objects: their "supertemporality proves to be omnitemporality, as correlate of an arbitrary producibility and reproducibility at any time-position" (Hua I 156). The in-itself of ideal objects is the "I can always again" without temporal limit. The line Husserl draws runs between this in-itself and the one he refuses. §41 rejects inferences to "allegedly in principle unknowable *things in themselves*" (*Dinge an sich*, Hua I 118), and §64 excludes only the metaphysics "operating with countersensical things in themselves" (Hua I 182). The quoted "in itself" of §27 is in itself because it can always again be made evident. The things in themselves are rejected because nothing could ever make them evident.

*Formal and Transcendental Logic*, from the same years, sets the logician's phrase in the same quotation marks. Its §81 opens with "the sense of the logical 'truth in itself' (*„Wahrheit an sich“*) or of 'objective truth'" as the theme of a critique (read in the machine-converted, unreviewed `husserl-hua-17-…` file, which the corpus caveats). There the in-itself is a presupposition of formal logic, to be examined. §27 gives the examination's result in a sentence. The in-itself is the correlate of an infinity of possible evidences grounded in the transcendental ego, and §27 also gives the reason why no single evidence could ever be enough.

## §28, Hua I 96–97: the world as correlate-idea of a perfect experiential evidence

Reads [§28, Paragraph 1](../commentaries/husserl/husserliana-1-cartesianische-meditationen-commentary.md#presumptive-evidence-of-world-experience-the-world-as-a-correlate-idea-of-perfect-experiential-evidence).

The world is an idea. This does not make it a thought in place of a thing. The real object, and the world as a whole, can have sense only as the unity that a perfect experiential evidence would give as itself. That evidence would be the complete synthesis of all possible experiences of it, and it is itself only an idea, since no actual synthesis of outer experience can be closed. The world is therefore a "correlate-idea": the idea that corresponds to the idea of a perfect evidence. Its transcendence is not a distance between consciousness and a thing beyond it. It is the structure of an experience that perfects itself without end and can always fail. That structure is constituted in consciousness and disclosed by explicating its horizons.

> In yet another way, evidences refer to infinities of evidences regarding the same object, and in a far more complicated way: namely always where they bring their object to self-givenness in an essential one-sidedness. This concerns nothing less than the whole of the evidences through which a real objective world, as a whole and in respect of any single objects, is immediately intuitively there for us. The evidence belonging to them is *outer experience*, and it is to be seen as an essential necessity that for objects of this kind no other manner of self-giving is conceivable. On the other hand, it is also to be seen that to this kind of evidence there belongs by essence *one-sidedness*, more precisely, a many-shaped horizon of unfulfilled but fulfilment-requiring anticipations, hence contents of mere meaning that refer to corresponding potential evidences. This imperfection of the evidence perfects itself in the realizing synthetic transitions from evidence to evidence, but necessarily in such a way that no conceivable synthesis of this kind is closed into an adequate evidence; rather, it always again carries with it unfulfilled pre-meanings and co-meanings.
>
> At the same time it always remains open that the belief in being that reaches into the anticipation is not fulfilled, that what appears in the mode *it itself* nevertheless is not, or is otherwise. Nonetheless outer experience is by essence the only persisting force, but of course only so long as experience, passively or actively running on, has the form of the synthesis of concordance. That the being of the world is in this way *transcendent* to consciousness, even in self-giving evidence, and necessarily remains transcendent, changes nothing in the fact that it is conscious life alone in which every transcendent constitutes itself as inseparable from it, and which, specifically as world-consciousness, inseparably carries in itself the sense world and also *this* actually existing world. Ultimately it is the disclosure of the horizons of experience alone that clarifies the actuality of the world and its *transcendence*, and then shows it to be inseparable from the transcendental subjectivity that constitutes sense and actuality of being. The reference, from every worldly experience, to concordant infinities of further possible experience — where after all an actually existing object can have sense only as a unity meant and to be meant in the connection of consciousness, a unity that would be given as itself in a perfect experiential evidence — evidently signifies that an actual object of a world, and all the more a world itself, is an infinite idea related to infinities of experiences to be concordantly united: a correlate-idea to the idea of a perfect experiential evidence, of a complete synthesis of possible experiences.

German:

> Noch in einer anderen Weise verweisen Evidenzen auf Unendlichkeiten von Evidenzen hinsichtlich desselben Gegenstandes, und in sehr viel komplizierterer Weise, nämlich stets da, wo sie ihren Gegenstand in einer wesensmäßigen Einseitigkeit zur Selbstgegebenheit bringen. Das betrifft nichts minderes als die gesamten Evidenzen, durch die für uns eine reale objektive Welt als ganze und nach irgendwelchen Einzelobjekten unmittelbar anschaulich da ist. Die ihnen zugehörige Evidenz ist die *äußere Erfahrung*, und es ist als Wesensnotwendigkeit einzusehen, daß für derartige Gegenstände keine andere Weise der Selbstgebung denkbar ist. Andererseits ist aber auch einzusehen, daß zu dieser Evidenzart wesensmäßig die *Einseitigkeit*, genauer gesprochen, ein vielgestaltiger Horizont unerfüllter, aber erfüllungsbedürftiger Antizipationen gehört, also Gehalte bloßer Meinung, die auf entsprechende potentielle Evidenzen verweisen. Diese Unvollkommenheit der Evidenz vervollkommnet sich in den verwirklichenden synthetischen Übergängen von Evidenz zu Evidenz, aber notwendig so, daß keine erdenkliche solche Synthesis zu einer adäquaten Evidenz abgeschlossen ist, vielmehr immer wieder unerfüllte Vormeinungen und Mitmeinungen mit sich führt.
>
> Zugleich bleibt es immer offen, daß der in die Antizipation hinreichende Seinsglaube sich nicht erfüllt, daß das im Modus des *Es selbst* Erscheinende nun doch nicht ist, oder anders ist. Gleichwohl ist äußere Erfahrung wesensmäßig die einzige beharrende Kraft, aber freilich nur so lange, als die passiv oder aktiv fortlaufende Erfahrung die Form der Synthesis der Einstimmigkeit hat. Daß das Sein der Welt in dieser Art dem Bewußtsein, und auch in der selbstgebenden Evidenz, *transzendent* ist und notwendig transzendent bleibt, ändert nichts daran, daß es das Bewußtseinsleben allein ist, in dem jedes Transzendente als von ihm Unabtrennbares sich konstituiert, und das speziell und als Weltbewußtsein in sich unabtrennbar den Sinn Welt und auch *diese* wirklich seiende Welt trägt. Letztlich ist es die Enthüllung der Erfahrungshorizonte allein, die die Wirklichkeit der Welt und ihre *Transzendenz* klärt und sie dann als von der Sinn und Seinswirklichkeit konstituierenden transzendentalen Subjektivität untrennbar erweist. Die Verweisung auf einstimmige Unendlichkeiten weiterer möglicher Erfahrung von jeder weltlichen Erfahrung aus, wo doch wirklich seiendes Objekt nur Sinn haben kann als im Bewußtseinszusammenhang vermeinte und zu vermeinende Einheit, die als sie selbst in einer vollkommenen Erfahrungsevidenz gegeben wäre, besagt offenbar, daß wirkliches Objekt einer Welt und erst recht eine Welt selbst eine unendliche, auf Unendlichkeiten einstimmig zu vereinender Erfahrungen bezogene Idee ist — eine Korrelatidee zur Idee einer vollkommenen Erfahrungsevidenz, einer vollständigen Synthesis möglicher Erfahrungen.

*Note on the text.* The two paragraphs are the whole of §28, and they run across the page break at Hua I 96/97 without loss. The apparatus has no note to this section. The §29 damage at Hua I 97 (src L2058, two sentences run together) begins in the next section and does not touch this one. The commentary's single block gives the first paragraph down to "anticipations" and then the last sentence. It leaves out the rest of the first paragraph and all of the second paragraph before its last sentence.

"In yet another way" (*Noch in einer anderen Weise*) sets §28 against §27. There the infinity of evidences was one of repetition: the same recollected datum, given again and again. Here the infinity comes from "essential one-sidedness" (*wesensmäßigen Einseitigkeit*). Two claims of essential necessity frame it. For real objects "no other manner of self-giving is conceivable" than outer experience, and to outer experience one-sidedness belongs "by essence". Together they block the thought that the thing could be given more fully in some other way and that outer experience falls short of that. One-sidedness is not a defect measured against a better access. It is the only way a real thing can be itself given. "More precisely" (*genauer gesprochen*) turns the defect into a structure: "a many-shaped horizon of unfulfilled but fulfilment-requiring anticipations". The adjective *erfüllungsbedürftig*, needing fulfilment, gives the anticipations a direction. They call for the experiences that would fulfil them.

The next sentence sets up the idea in its verb. "This imperfection of the evidence perfects itself" (*Diese Unvollkommenheit der Evidenz vervollkommnet sich*): the noun and the verb share a root, so imperfection is the subject of perfecting. The perfecting happens "necessarily in such a way that no conceivable synthesis of this kind is closed into an adequate evidence". The word "closed" (*abgeschlossen*) recalls §22's "relatively closed" theories. Perfecting without closure is exactly what an idea in the sense of §22 regulates. The goal, a perfect evidence, is anticipated in every step and reached in none.

The second paragraph adds what the commentary omits, the possibility of failure. "It always remains open" that the belief in being "is not fulfilled", that what appears in the mode "*it itself*" "nevertheless is not, or is otherwise". "Is not" is the crossed-out being of §23, and "is otherwise" is the correction of one sense by another. Outer experience is still "the only persisting force" (*die einzige beharrende Kraft*), "but of course only so long as" experience keeps "the form of the synthesis of concordance". The world persists on a condition, and the condition is concordance (*Einstimmigkeit*). Two uses of "alone" (*allein*) then fix the transcendental claim. Transcendence "changes nothing in the fact that it is conscious life alone" in which every transcendent is constituted, and "ultimately it is the disclosure of the horizons of experience alone" that clarifies the world's actuality and its transcendence. The world is transcendent even in self-giving evidence. Its transcendence is clarified only from within, by explicating the horizons that make it transcendent.

The last sentence draws the conclusion, and its connectives claim more than the argument shows. Its premise is a concessive clause, "where after all" (*wo doch*), which brings in §§26–27's thesis as already settled: a real object can have sense only as the unity that "would be given as itself in a perfect experiential evidence". The subjunctive "would be given" (*gegeben wäre*) marks that evidence as counterfactual. From the referral to concordant infinities together with that premise, the reference "evidently signifies" (*besagt offenbar*) that the object, "and all the more" (*erst recht*) the world, is an infinite idea. "Evidently" (*offenbar*) covers a real step. The horizon structure shows that no synthesis closes. That the object *is* nothing but the correlate of the unclosable synthesis needs the premise, and the premise is the transcendental idealism that §41 will claim as phenomenology's own result. The formula that ends the sentence is symmetrical. The world is "a correlate-idea to the idea of a perfect experiential evidence" (*eine Korrelatidee zur Idee*). Both sides are ideas. The object is an idea because the evidence that would give it is one.

The *Paris Lectures* have the structure without the word. There the real thing's being refers me, "by virtue of the presumptive horizon, to an endlessly open system of possible perceptions as such, which are not invented, but motivated in my intentional life", and which keep their validity until conflicting experience "cancels" it. The lecture adds at once, "but of course, that is all very roughly spoken" (*Pariser Vorträge*, Hua I 23–24, src L917–921). The Meditations replace the rough sketch with the vocabulary of §22. The open system becomes an "infinite idea", and "not invented" (*nicht erfunden*) returns in §22's parenthesis, "not an invention" (*nicht eine Erfindung*). §28 applies to the world what §22 said of the system of all objects of possible consciousness, and §29 draws the methodological consequence. To produce the absolute evidence of a real object "is a senseless goal". The task is to clarify its essential structure, the "infinity-dimensions" that "systematically build up its ideal infinite synthesis" (Hua I 98).

The Fifth Meditation splits the idea in two. The primordial world, my own world before others, is a transcendence that, "notwithstanding its ideality as synthetic unity of an infinite system of my potentialities", is still a determining part of my concrete being as ego (§48, Hua I 136). The objective world is "the objective world as *idea*, as ideal correlate of an intersubjective experience" to be carried through concordantly without end, related to an intersubjectivity "constituted in the ideality of endless openness" (§49, Hua I 138). §28's perfect experiential evidence, which no single subject could achieve, becomes there the idea of a communalized experience. The correlate-idea keeps its form, and its subject becomes plural. At §28 the world is the correlate of an evidence that is itself only an idea. Its transcendence is the gap between every actual synthesis and that idea, and the conditional "so long as" concordance lasts keeps the gap open.

## §33, Hua I 102–103: the monad, "with Leibniz's word"

Reads [§33, Paragraph 1](../commentaries/husserl/husserliana-1-cartesianische-meditationen-commentary.md#the-full-concretion-of-the-ego-as-monad-and-the-problem-of-its-self-constitution).

"Monad" names the ego in full concretion: the ego together with everything without which it could not concretely be. That is more than a pole of acts and more than a substrate of habits. It is the whole streaming life of consciousness and, with it, the objects meant in that life, including the familiar surrounding world that the ego has made its own through earlier acts. The monad therefore contains its world, as meant and as acquired, and for that reason its self-constitution is the whole of phenomenology. Husserl takes the word from Leibniz and says so, but what he borrows is a word for concretion. The doctrines the word carries in Leibniz (innateness, compossibility, mirroring, harmony) come into the Meditations later, one at a time, and each is redefined when it arrives.

> From the I as identical pole and as substrate of habitualities we distinguish the ego taken in full concretion (which we wish to call, with the Leibnizian word, monad), by adding that without which the I just cannot be concretely; namely, it can be only in the streaming many-shapedness of its intentional life and in the objects meant in it and perhaps constituting themselves for it as existing. Evidently, for these objects the character, in each case, of abiding being and being-so is a correlate of the habituality of its position-takings, which constitutes itself in the I-pole itself.
>
> That is to be understood as follows. As ego I have a continually *existing-for-me* surrounding world, and in it objects as existing for me: namely, either already, in abiding articulation, as known to me, or only anticipated as to be got to know. The former, those existing for me in the first sense, are so from original experience, that is, from the original taking-cognizance, the explication in particular intuitions of what was at first never seen. Through this the object constitutes itself in my synthetic activity in the explicit sense-form *identical of its manifold properties*, hence as object identical with itself, as determining itself in its manifold properties. This activity of mine of positing being and explicating being institutes a habituality of my I, by virtue of which this object is now abidingly my own as the object of its determinations. Such abiding acquisitions constitute my known surrounding world in each case, with its horizon of unknown objects, that is, objects still to be acquired, anticipated in advance with this formal object-structure.
>
> I am for myself, and am continually given to myself through experiential evidence as *I myself*. This holds for the transcendental ego (but also, in the parallel, for the psychologically pure one), and in every sense of *ego*. Since the monadically concrete ego comprises the whole actual and potential conscious life, it is clear that the problem of the phenomenological explication of this monadic ego (the problem of its constitution for itself) must comprise in itself all constitutive problems whatever. In further consequence, the phenomenology of this self-constitution coincides with phenomenology as a whole.

German:

> Vom Ich als identischem Pol und als Substrat von Habitualitäten unterscheiden wir das in voller Konkretion genommene ego (das wir mit dem Leibniz’schen Worte Monade nennen wollen), indem wir hinzunehmen, ohne was das Ich eben konkret nicht sein kann; nämlich das kann es nur sein in der strömenden Vielgestaltigkeit seines intentionalen Lebens und den darin vermeinten und ev. als seiend für es sich konstituierenden Gegenständen. Offenbar ist für diese der jeweilige Charakter bleibenden Seins und So-seins ein Korrelat der im Ichpol selbst sich konstituierenden Habitualität seiner Stellungnahme.
>
> Das ist so zu verstehen. Ich habe als ego eine fortwährend *für-mich-seiende* Umwelt, in ihr Gegenstände als für mich seiende, nämlich schon in bleibender Gliederung für mich als bekannte, oder nur antizipiert als kennenzulernende. Die ersteren, die im ersten Sinne für mich seienden, sind es aus ursprünglicher Erfahrung, das ist der ursprünglichen Kenntnisnahme, der Explikation des zunächst nie Erblickten in Sonderanschauungen. Dadurch konstituiert sich in meiner synthetischen Aktivität der Gegenstand in der expliziten Sinnesform *Identisches seiner mannigfaltigen Eigenschaften*, also Gegenstand als mit sich Identisches, als in seinen mannigfaltigen Eigenschaften sich Bestimmendes. Diese meine Aktivität der Seinssetzung und Seinsauslegung stiftet eine Habitualität meines Ich, vermöge deren mir nun dieser Gegenstand als der seiner Bestimmungen bleibend zueigen ist. Solche bleibenden Erwerbe konstituieren meine jeweilige bekannte Umwelt mit ihrem Horizont unbekannter Gegenstände, das ist noch zu erwerbender, im voraus antizipiert mit dieser formalen Gegenstandsstruktur.
>
> Ich bin für mich selbst und mir immerfort durch Erfahrungs-evidenz als *Ich selbst* gegeben. Das gilt für das transzendentale ego (aber auch in der Parallele für das psychologisch reine), und in jedem Sinne von *ego*. Da das monadisch konkrete ego das gesamte wirkliche und potentielle Bewußtseinsleben mit befaßt, so ist es klar, daß das Problem der phänomenologischen Auslegung dieses monadischen ego (das Problem seiner Konstitution für sich selbst) alle konstitutiven Probleme überhaupt in sich befassen muß. In weiterer Folge ergibt sich die Deckung der Phänomenologie dieser Selbstkonstitution mit der Phänomenologie überhaupt.

*Note on the text.* The section runs from Hua I 102 into 103; the page markers are a bare one before §33 and "103" before its third paragraph, and the text is continuous. "Erfahrungs-evidenz" is hyphenated in the file mid-line, probably a print line-break. The commentary's block gives the first sentence and the last two. It leaves out the second sentence of the first paragraph, the whole second paragraph, and the first two sentences of the third.

The borrowing is marked as a decision about a word. Husserl writes that "we wish to call" (*nennen wollen*) the concrete ego a monad "with the Leibnizian word" (*mit dem Leibniz'schen Worte*). He does not say that the ego is what Leibniz called a monad. The *Paris Lectures* had hedged the same way with a different verb, "to speak with Leibniz" (*mit Leibniz zu sprechen*), when they called the concrete ego "my monad" (*Pariser Vorträge*, Hua I 28). The definition that the parenthesis interrupts says what the word is for. Concretion is reached "by adding that without which the I just cannot be concretely" (*ohne was das Ich eben konkret nicht sein kann*). This is the language of dependent and independent parts. The pole of §31 and the substrate of habitualities of §32 are abstractions, moments that cannot stand alone. The monad is the smallest whole that can. The list of what must be added carries the weight. Besides the "streaming many-shapedness" of intentional life it includes "the objects meant in it and perhaps constituting themselves for it as existing". The monad contains objects, even those whose being is only "perhaps" (*ev.*, eventually) constituted. A soul-substance holds its representations. This monad holds what the representations are of, as meant.

The second sentence turns this into a correlation, and the commentary does not keep it. The "abiding being and being-so" of the objects is "a correlate of the habituality" of the ego's position-takings. The middle paragraph explains it with the vocabulary of property. The surrounding world is "*existing-for-me*" (*für-mich-seiende*, italic and hyphenated into one predicate). Its known objects are known "from original experience", from the explication of "what was at first never seen". The activity of positing and explicating "institutes" (*stiftet*) a habituality, "by virtue of which this object is now abidingly my own" (*bleibend zueigen*). The known world is made of "abiding acquisitions" (*bleibende Erwerbe*), and the unknown is "still to be acquired" (*noch zu erwerbender*). This is §27's language (an evidence "institutes" a "lasting possession") transferred from the evidence to the ego. The known world belongs to the monad as its acquired property, and its horizon of the unknown is property not yet acquired. The word *zueigen* (one's own) belongs to the family of *eigen* that the Fifth Meditation will make its theme as the "sphere of ownness" (*Eigenheitssphäre*). The monad of §33 already has a world that is its own, and §44 will have to reduce to it.

The last paragraph draws the consequence in two steps. "I am for myself, and am continually given to myself through experiential evidence as *I myself*." Self-experience is the evidence the monad has of itself, and it holds "in every sense of *ego*", transcendental and psychological. Then: since the monad "comprises the whole actual and potential conscious life", the problem of its self-constitution "must comprise all constitutive problems whatever", and "in further consequence" phenomenology of self-constitution and phenomenology as such "coincide" (*Deckung*). The step is valid only because the first paragraph put the objects inside the monad. If the monad held only acts, the constitution of objects would be another problem. Because it holds its meant world, every constitutive problem is a problem of the monad's constitution of itself. The commentary keeps the conclusion and drops the premise that makes it follow.

§33 opens the Fourth Meditation's turn from the constituted object to the constituting ego. §§30–32 had built the ego up in layers: inseparable from its experiences, pole of its acts, substrate of habitualities. §33 gives the whole and names it. §34 then makes the analysis eidetic, so that the monad becomes the eidos ego, and §§36–39 lay out its essential laws. The "coincidence" also states the difficulty that §13 had postponed. If all phenomenology is the self-explication of one monad, the charge of transcendental solipsism is close. §41 will call this self-explication transcendental idealism, and §42 opens with the objection itself.

The Leibnizian doctrines then arrive separately, and each is given a phenomenological sense. Compossibility enters in §36: not all possible types of experience "are *compossible*" in one possible ego (Hua I 108). The innate enters in §39, where association is "a realm of the *innate* a priori, without which an *ego* as such is unthinkable" (Hua I 114). Descartes' "principles innate to the ego", from which he meant to infer the rest of the world (§10, Hua I 63), are thus replaced by an a priori of genesis. Mirroring enters in §44. The other ego constitutes itself as "*mirroring* itself" in "my monad", and Husserl at once withdraws half the word: "the Other is a mirroring of myself, and yet not properly a mirroring; an analogue of myself, and yet again not an analogue in the usual sense" (Hua I 125). Harmony enters in §49, with a denial: the "harmony of the monads" is not meant as a "*metaphysical* substruction", "no more than the monads themselves are metaphysical inventions or hypotheses" (Hua I 138). In §60 the name returns as a judgment: "Of course Leibniz is right" that infinitely many monads are conceivable but not all compossible (Hua I 167; row 28 reads the passage). §62 then answers the charge that the step to the other was possible only through "a secret takeover of Leibnizian traditions" (Hua I 175). Phenomenological idealism "turned out to be a monadology which, with all its intentional echoes of Leibniz's metaphysics, draws its content purely from the phenomenological explication of transcendental experience" (*bei allen absichtlichen Anklängen an Leibnizens Metaphysik*, Hua I 176–177).

"Intentional echoes" is the right measure for §33. The echoes are deliberate, and each one is tested against what phenomenological explication can show. The word that §33 takes over is the one that fits first: an individual that contains its own world as represented. In §33 that world is meant and acquired, its own and still to be acquired. The other Leibnizian terms join the word only when the analysis reaches them, and the monad's concretion, which includes its world, is what makes the whole of phenomenology its self-explication.

## §34, Hua I 105: the eidos floating "in the air of pure conceivabilities"

Reads [§34, Paragraph 2](../commentaries/husserl/husserliana-1-cartesianische-meditationen-commentary.md#principled-elaboration-of-the-phenomenological-method-transcendental-analysis-as-eidetic).

Eidetic variation frees a type from the fact it was found in. Starting from an actual perception, the phenomenologist alters it at will, in phantasy, holding fast only that it remain a perception of something, and so turns the fact into one pure possibility among endlessly many. What stays invariant through the variation is the eidos, here the eidos *perception*, and its extension is all conceivable perceptions. The eidos is no longer bound to any fact, not even to the factual ego that does the varying. Husserl's figure for this release is that the type "floats so to speak in the air". The figure carries the claim, and its hedge marks its limit. The eidos is not grounded in any actuality, yet it is reached only from an actual example and by an actual ego's phantasy, and it is "seen", not invented.

> Let us make this clear and then methodically fruitful. Starting from the example of this table-perception, we vary the perceptual object table in a completely free arbitrariness, yet so that we hold fast perception as perception of something — of something, no matter what — beginning, say, by refashioning in fiction quite arbitrarily its shape, its colour and so on, holding identically fast only the perceptual appearing. In other words, we transform the fact of this perception, under abstention from its being-validity, into a pure possibility, and among other quite arbitrary pure possibilities — but pure possibilities of perceptions. We transpose, as it were, the actual perception into the realm of non-actualities, of the as-if, which supplies us with the pure possibilities, pure of everything that binds to the fact and to every fact whatever. In the latter respect we also do not keep these possibilities bound to the co-posited factual ego, but precisely as completely free conceivability of phantasy — so that we could also have taken from the outset, as starting example, a phantasying of ourselves into a perceiving, outside all relation to the rest of our factual life. The general type *perception* thus gained floats, so to speak, in the air — in the air of absolutely pure conceivabilities. Thus lifted out of all facticity, it has become the eidos *perception*, whose *ideal* extension is made up of all ideally possible perceptions as pure conceivabilities. The perception-analyses are then essential analyses; everything we have set out about the syntheses belonging to the type perception, about horizons of potentiality and so on, holds, as is easily seen, by essence for everything that can be formed in this free variation, hence for all conceivable perceptions whatever, in other words in absolute essential generality, and with essential necessity for every single case picked out, hence also for every factual perception, inasmuch as every fact is to be thought as a mere example of a pure possibility.

German:

> Machen wir uns das klar und dann methodisch fruchtbar. Ausgehend vom Exempel dieser Tischwahrnehmung variieren wir den Wahrnehmungsgegenstand Tisch in einem völlig freien Belieben, jedoch so, daß wir Wahrnehmung als Wahrnehmung von etwas — von etwas, beliebig was — festhalten, etwa anfangend damit, daß wir seine Gestalt, die Farbe usw. ganz willkürlich umfingieren, nur identisch festhaltend das wahrnehmungsmäßige Erscheinen. Mit anderen Worten, wir verwandeln das Faktum dieser Wahrnehmung unter Enthaltung von ihrer Seinsgeltung in eine reine Möglichkeit und unter anderen ganz beliebigen reinen Möglichkeiten — aber reinen Möglichkeiten von Wahrnehmungen. Wir versetzen gleichsam die wirkliche Wahrnehmung in das Reich der Unwirklichkeiten, des Als-ob, das uns die reinen Möglichkeiten liefert, rein von allem, was an das Faktum und jedes Faktum überhaupt bindet. In letzterer Hinsicht behalten wir diese Möglichkeiten auch nicht in Bindung an das mitgesetzte faktische ego, sondern eben als völlig freie Erdenklichkeit der Phantasie — so daß wir auch von vornherein als Ausgangsexempel ein Hineinphantasieren in ein Wahrnehmen hätten nehmen können, außer aller Beziehung zu unserem sonstigen faktischen Leben. Der so gewonnene allgemeine Typus *Wahrnehmung* schwebt sozusagen in der Luft — in der Luft absolut reiner Erdenklichkeiten. So aller Faktizität enthoben, ist er zum Eidos *Wahrnehmung* geworden, dessen *idealen* Umfang alle idealiter möglichen Wahrnehmungen als reine Erdenklichkeiten ausmachen. Die Wahrnehmungsanalysen sind dann Wesensanalysen, alles was wir über die zum Typus Wahrnehmung gehörigen Synthesen, über Horizonte der Potentialität usw. ausgeführt haben, gilt, wie leicht ersichtlich, wesensmäßig für alles in dieser freien Variation zu Bildende, also für alle erdenklichen Wahrnehmungen überhaupt, mit anderen Worten in absoluter Wesensallgemeinheit und für jeden herausgegriffenen Einzelfall in Wesensnotwendigkeit, also auch für jede faktische Wahrnehmung, sofern jedes Faktum als bloßes Exempel einer reinen Möglichkeit zu denken ist.

*Note on the text.* The paragraph begins at the top of Hua I 105 and is complete. Its first part is one long line in the file, and the end ("zum Typus Wahrnehmung gehörigen … zu denken ist") keeps the print's short lines; nothing is lost between them. The apparatus has no note to pp. 103–105.

The verbs of the passage move the fact step by step out of actuality. The table's shape and colour are "refashioned in fiction" (*umfingieren*), a verb for altering by feigning. The fact is "transformed" into a pure possibility "under abstention from its being-validity" (*unter Enthaltung von ihrer Seinsgeltung*). That is the *epochē*'s word (§8, §23) applied a second time. The reduction abstained from the world's being; variation abstains from the being of the example itself. The perception is then "transposed" into "the realm of non-actualities, of the as-if" (*des Als-ob*). This is the work's own name for phantasy. §25 had called phantasy "actuality-as-if" and quasi-positionality "of the as-if" (Hua I 94). Eidetic method is thus founded on the neutral mode that §22 had refused to the regulative idea ("not an 'as if'"). The two idealities of the Meditations are distinguished by this mode. The eidos is won in the as-if, and the idea of the system of objects is posited and anticipated.

Each step of release is hedged. "As it were" (*gleichsam*) qualifies the transposition into the as-if, and "so to speak" (*sozusagen*) qualifies the floating. The hedges matter because the passage claims a total release, "pure of everything that binds to the fact and to every fact whatever", and the next sentence pushes it further. The possibilities are not kept bound "to the co-posited factual ego" either. The example could have been a "phantasying of ourselves into a perceiving" (*Hineinphantasieren*), with no relation to our factual life. Even the phenomenologist's own existence drops out of what the eidos depends on. Then the figure: the type "floats, so to speak, in the air — in the air of absolutely pure conceivabilities" (*schwebt sozusagen in der Luft*). The dash repeats "in the air" in order to specify it. Floating would ordinarily mean having no support. The repetition gives the eidos an element of its own to float in, "pure conceivabilities" (*Erdenklichkeiten*). "Lifted out of all facticity" (*aller Faktizität enthoben*) completes the movement. The eidos has no ground in fact, and the sentence presents that as its freedom rather than as a lack.

The last sentence states what is gained, and its connectives carry the method's claim. What was said of perception "holds, as is easily seen, by essence" for everything formed in the variation, "hence" for all conceivable perceptions, "in other words" in absolute generality, "and" with necessity for each case, "hence also" for every factual perception. The chain ends where variation began. The fact comes back as "a mere example of a pure possibility" (*bloßes Exempel*). "Mere" reverses the order of priority. The table-perception was the starting point, and now it is an instance. "As is easily seen" (*wie leicht ersichtlich*) passes over a real question, the one the hedges already raised. The variation is run by a factual ego from a factual example, so it is not obvious that the result is free of every fact. Husserl answers it only in the next paragraph. Variation is meant "as evident", self-giving the possibilities "in pure intuition", and the eidos is "a seen or seeable universal", "conditioned by no fact" "according to its own intuitive sense" (Hua I 105). Freedom from fact is claimed for the eidos's sense, not for the act that reaches it.

§34.1 explains why the eidetic turn comes so late. The descriptions so far were meant to work first "in the simpler garb of a merely empirical description", and expressions such as "essential necessity" had "for good reasons" kept forcing themselves on it (Hua I 103). §34.2 shows what those expressions meant all along, and the rest of the section draws the consequence. Every type lifted "into the pure essential sphere" keeps its outer horizons, now eidetic, so we stand "not in the factual ego but in an eidos ego". The descriptions of the Second and Third Meditations were "in fact back-translations from the original eidetic shape into that of an empirical typology" (Hua I 105). Everything from §35 on is eidetic: the universe of compossible forms (§36), genesis (§§37–39), and the Fifth Meditation's constitution of the other.

The figure returns once, negated, in the Fifth Meditation. In §60 Husserl asks whether two separate communities of monads, constituting two separate worlds, are conceivable, and he calls the thought "a pure countersense" instead of a conceivability. "For the two intersubjectivities do not stand in the air; as thought up by me, they both stand in necessary community with me as the constituting primal monad for them (or with me in a possible variation of myself)" (*Denn die beiden Intersubjektivitäten stehen nicht in der Luft*, Hua I 167). Here the limit that §34's hedges marked becomes explicit. An eidos of perception may float in the air of pure conceivabilities. Possible worlds of monads may not, because whatever I can conceive I conceive as a variation of myself, the factual ego from which all variation starts. §34 had said the same in the parenthesis on the eidos ego, "a pure possible variation of my factual one". "So to speak" is what the hedge was for: the eidos floats only relative to the facts it no longer depends on, never relative to the ego whose variation it is.

The *Paris Lectures* state the method without the figure. "Evidently I can refashion my ego quite freely in fiction (*umfingieren*), can regard the types as purely ideal possibilities of the now merely possible ego and of a possible ego in general (as free variation of my factual one)" (*Pariser Vorträge*, Hua I 28, src L992). The verb is the same, and the variation is applied to the ego at once. The Meditations start instead from a table-perception, add the as-if and the air, and keep the qualification "as free variation of my factual one" in the eidos ego. The eidos floats in the air of pure conceivabilities, but the air is that of the ego's own possible variations.

## §37, Hua I 109–110: motivation, genesis and the ego's "so to speak" history

Reads [§37, Paragraph 1](../commentaries/husserl/husserliana-1-cartesianische-meditationen-commentary.md#time-as-the-universal-form-of-all-egological-genesis).

The ego's experiences hang together by law, but the law is not causal. It is motivation: one experience occurs because of others, as their sense calls it forth, and the whole stream is held together by "all-linking" motivation. The most general form of that motivation is time, the form in which past, present and future are constituted together. Inside this form particular motivations produce particular acquisitions, and their lawful sequence is the ego's genesis. Since every constitution of objects belongs to the ego's self-constitution (§33), every constitutive system the ego has is the outcome of a genesis. The ego, Husserl says, constitutes itself "so to speak in the unity of a *history*". "History" is the word for genesis seen from the ego's side: a lawful becoming in which what was once achieved remains as habit. "So to speak" keeps the word from meaning the history of events that historians tell.

> The essential laws of compossibility (in the fact, rules of being-and-being-able-to-be-together-at-once-or-in-succession) are in a widest sense laws of causality — laws for an If and So. But it is better here to avoid the prejudice-laden expression causality and to speak in the transcendental sphere (as in the purely psychological) of **motivation**. The universe of experiences that make up the really inherent being-content of the transcendental *ego* is a compossible one only in the universal **unity-form of streaming**, into which all particulars order themselves as themselves streaming in it. So already this most general form of all particular forms of concrete experiences, and of the formations constituted streamingly in their streaming itself, is a form of all-linking *motivation*, ruling in each particular in particular, which we can also address as a **formal lawfulness of a universal genesis**, according to which past, present and future constitute themselves in one, again and again, in a certain noetic-noematic form-structure of streaming modes of givenness. But within this form life runs its course as a motivated progression of particular constituting achievements with manifold particular motivations and systems of motivation, which according to general lawfulnesses of genesis produce a unity of the universal genesis of the *ego*. The *ego* constitutes itself for itself, so to speak, in the unity of a *history*; and if we have said that in the constitution of the *ego* all constitutions of all objectivities existing for it, immanent and transcendent, ideal and real, are included, it is now to be added that the constitutive systems through which these and those objects and categories of objects are for the *ego* are themselves possible only in the frame of a lawful genesis. At the same time they are bound by the universal genetic form that makes the concrete *ego* (the monad) possible as a unity, as compossible in its particular being-content. That for me there is a nature, a cultural world, a human world with its social forms and so on, signifies that possibilities of corresponding experiences exist for me — as ones I can at any time bring into play and freely continue in a certain synthetic style, whether or not I am actually experiencing such objects; further, that other modes of consciousness corresponding to them, vague meanings and the like, exist as possibilities for me, and that possibilities also belong to them of being fulfilled or disappointed by experiences of a prefigured typology. In this lies a firmly formed habituality — a formed one, acquired from a certain genesis standing under essential laws.

German:

> Die Wesensgesetze der Kompossibilität (im Faktum Regeln des Miteinander-zugleich-oder-folgend-zu-sein und -sein-zu-können) sind in einem weitesten Sinne Gesetze der Kausalität — Gesetze für ein Wenn und So. Doch ist es hier besser, den vorurteilsbelasteten Ausdruck Kausalität zu vermeiden und in der transzendentalen Sphäre (wie in der rein-psychologischen) von **Motivation** zu sprechen. Das Universum der Erlebnisse, die den reellen Seinsgehalt des transzendentalen *ego* ausmachen, ist ein kompossibles nur in der universalen **Einheitsformdes Strömens**, in welche alle Einzelheiten selbst als darin strömende sich einordnen. Also schon diese allgemeinsten Form aller Sonderformen von konkreten Erlebnissen und der in ihrem Strömen selbst strömend konstituierten Gebilde ist eine Form allverknüpfender und in jeder Einzelheit insonderheit waltender *Motivation*, die wir auch mit ansprechen können als eine **formale Gesetzmäßigkeiteineruniversalen Genesis**, der gemäß sich immer wieder in einer gewissen noetisch-noematischen Formstruktur strömender Gegebenheitsweisen Vergangenheit, Gegenwart und Zukunft in eins konstituiert. Aber innerhalb dieser Form verläuft das Leben als ein motivierter Gang besonderer konstituierender Leistungen mit vielfältigen besonderen Motivationen und Motivationssystemen, die nach allgemeinen Gesetzmäßigkeiten der Genesis eine Einheit der universalen Genesis des *ego* herstellen. Das *ego* konstituiert sich für sich selbst sozusagen in der Einheit einer *Geschichte*, und wenn wir gesagt haben, daß in der Konstitution des *ego* alle Konstitutionen aller für es seienden Gegenständlichkeiten, immanenter wie transzendenter, idealer wie realer, beschlossen sind, so ist jetzt beizufügen, daß die konstitutiven Systeme, durch die für das *ego* die und jene Gegenstände und Gegenstandskategorien sind, selbst nur im Rahmen einer gesetzmäßigen Genesis möglich sind. Zugleich sind sie dabei gebunden durch die universale genetische Form, die das konkrete *ego* (die Monade) als Einheit, als in ihrem besonderen Seinsgehalt kompossibel möglich macht. Daß für mich eine Natur ist, eine Kulturwelt, eine Menschenwelt mit ihren sozialen Formen usw., besagt, daß Möglichkeiten entsprechender Erfahrungen für mich bestehen — als für mich jederzeit ins Spiel zu setzende, in gewissem synthetischen Stil frei fortzuführende, ob ich gerade solche Gegenstände wirklich erfahre oder nicht; in weiterer Folge, daß ihnen entsprechende andere Bewußtseinsmodi, vage Meinungen und dgl., als Möglichkeiten für mich sind und daß ihnen auch Möglichkeiten zugehören, sie durch Erfahrungen vorgezeichneter Typik zu erfüllen oder zu enttäuschen. Darin liegt eine fest ausgebildete Habitualität — eine ausgebildete, aus einer gewissen, unter Wesensgesetzen stehenden Genesis erworbene.

*Note on the text.* The paragraph is one line in the file. The file sets the marker for Hua I 110 just after the § heading, but the apparatus places this paragraph on pp. 109–110 (its notes run from 109,4 to 110,2, and 110,16 falls in the paragraph after next), so the file's markers run one page ahead here. The apparatus also records three later changes in Husserl's copy: "vom Naturalismus her" [from naturalism] inserted before "vorurteilsbelasteten" (109,4); "Geschichte, und wenn" changed to "Geschichte. Wenn", so that the history sentence ends with the word (109,25); and the constitutive systems renamed "Systeme der Vormöglichkeit" [systems of pre-possibility], through which objects are "Geltungseinheiten" [unities of validity] for the ego (109,29–31). The quotation gives the printed text. Two bold phrases run their words together ("**Einheitsformdes Strömens**", "**formale Gesetzmäßigkeiteineruniversalen Genesis**"); the emphasis covers each phrase, and nothing below rests on its word boundaries. "diese allgemeinsten Form" should grammatically be "diese allgemeinste Form"; the file has the slip and the translation follows the sense. The file marks "Motivation" in bold on its first use and in italics on its second. Whether the edition distinguishes the two cannot be told from the file; the reading takes both only as emphasis.

The paragraph opens by conceding a word and then setting it aside. The laws of compossibility, which §36 introduced, "are in a widest sense laws of causality — laws for an If and So" (*Gesetze für ein Wenn und So*). The concession is purely formal: any rule of the form "if this, then so" can be called causal. "But it is better here" (*Doch ist es hier besser*) uses the same move as §22's "But we say better". The correction keeps the content and changes the name, because the name brings a "prejudice" with it (*vorurteilsbelasteten*, "prejudice-laden"). The adjective ties the choice to §3's "fateful prejudice" and §7's "pre-judgments". Husserl's later insertion names the prejudice: the expression is prejudice-laden "from naturalism" (*vom Naturalismus her*, apparatus 109,4). "Causality" carries the natural attitude's picture of things acting on things in objective time. Applied to experiences, it would turn the stream into a natural process and the ego into a part of nature. "Motivation", set in bold, names a connection of sense. One experience occurs because another, as meant, calls it forth. The parenthesis "(as in the purely psychological)" keeps the term for the parallel psychology. Motivation is not a privilege of the transcendental attitude, but the attitude in which causality ceases to be the model.

The next two sentences put time at the base of motivation. The experiences that make up the "really inherent" (*reellen*) content of the ego are compossible "only in the universal unity-form of streaming". The form itself is "a form of all-linking *motivation*". Time is thus not a container in which motivations occur. It is the most general motivation, the "formal lawfulness of a universal genesis" by which past, present and future "constitute themselves in one, again and again". The word "genesis" is introduced here as another name for that form, "which we can also address as" (*die wir auch mit ansprechen können*). Inside it, "life runs its course as a motivated progression" (*motivierter Gang*) of particular achievements, and their motivations "produce" (*herstellen*) "a unity of the universal genesis of the *ego*". Two levels are distinguished: the formal genesis of time, which every ego has, and the material genesis of particular acquisitions, which makes each ego the one it is.

The sentence on history follows from that second level, and it is hedged. "The *ego* constitutes itself for itself, so to speak, in the unity of a *history*" (*sozusagen in der Einheit einer* Geschichte). The italic gives the word weight and "so to speak" holds it back. History in the ordinary sense is a sequence of events in objective time, told from outside. The ego's history is the order of its acquisitions, constituted "for itself", in which each achievement remains as a habit that conditions the next. "So to speak" marks the analogy and its limit. The commentary's version, "The ego constitutes itself for itself in the unity of a history", drops the hedge and states the analogy as an identity. Husserl's own revision went the other way: he made "*Geschichte*" end its sentence (109,25), giving the word a full stop of its own, and kept "so to speak". The rest of the sentence draws the consequence for §33's thesis. All constitutions of objects lie within the ego's constitution, and "it is now to be added" that the constitutive systems themselves are "possible only in the frame of a lawful genesis". A nature, a cultural world, a human world exist for me as possibilities of experience I can "bring into play at any time". That availability "is" a habituality, "acquired from a certain genesis standing under essential laws". The paragraph ends on the word "acquired" (*erworbene*), which returns to §33's "abiding acquisitions" (*bleibende Erwerbe*). The world I have is the deposit of my history.

§37 continues by distinguishing the "static" phenomenology done so far, whose descriptions are "analogous to those of natural history" (Hua I 110 by the apparatus; the file's marker says 111), from the genetic phenomenology now opened. §38 uses the word "history" twice more, both times in italics and both times without a hedge. The passive synthesis in which a thing appears "has, as synthesis of this form, its *history* announcing itself in it", and intentional references in the phenomena "lead back to a *history*", showing them as "after-formations" of earlier formations (Hua I 112–113 by the file's markers, which run a page ahead in this stretch). By §38, history has become a term of method: the sedimented sense that intentional analysis can read back from what is given. The "so to speak" of §37 is not repeated, but the history meant is still that of sense, not of events. §39 then names the principle of passive genesis, association, and §64 gives genesis its widest scope.

Ideas II had already used motivation as the alternative to causality, and with the same scare quotes. Motivation is "the lawfulness of spiritual life" (*Ideen II* §56, Hua IV 220), and the two kinds of motivation intertwine, "the 'causality' in the substrata of association and apperception and the 'causality' of reason, the passive and the active or free" (Hua IV 224, read in `husserl-hua-4-…`; the file prints "Allerings" for *Allerdings* and carries margin line numbers into the text). There motivation is set against the causality of nature and governs the person, the subject of the spiritual world. In §37 motivation is no longer one region's law opposed to another's. It is the form of the whole transcendental life, and time is its most general case. Ideas II put "causality" in quotation marks when using it of motivation. §37 drops the word with a reason, its prejudice, and keeps a hedge only on "history". The ego is a unity because its genesis is lawful, and it is the one ego it is because what it has achieved remains with it.

## §39, Hua I 113–114: association as a title of intentionality

Reads [§39, Paragraph 1](../commentaries/husserl/husserliana-1-cartesianische-meditationen-commentary.md#association-as-a-principle-of-passive-genesis).

Association is the principle of passive genesis. Through it everything that active thinking finds already given has been constituted, from the experiences of the stream itself to the things of nature. Husserl keeps the old name and changes the kind of thing it names. In the tradition that runs "since Hume", association is an empirical law by which data in a soul combine, a kind of attraction between mental atoms. For phenomenology it is "a *title of intentionality*": a lawful connection of sense, in which one given awakens another as belonging with it. Its laws are essential laws of the ego's constitution, not empirical regularities found in it, and so association belongs to the "innate" a priori without which an ego is not conceivable. The re-founding matters because the Fifth Meditation will explain the experience of others as an association, the pairing of my body with another. Only an association that is itself intentional, a connection of sense rather than a mechanism of data, could do that work.

> The universal principle of passive genesis for the constitution of all objectivities ultimately pregiven to active formation bears the title association. It is, be it noted, a *title of intentionality*: as such descriptively demonstrable in its primal forms, and in its intentional achievements standing under essential laws, from which each and every passive constitution is to be made intelligible, that of experiences as immanent temporal objects as much as that of all real natural objects of the objective spatiotemporal world. **Association is a transcendental-phenomenological basic concept** (as it is also, in the psychological parallel, a basic concept of a purely intentional psychology). The old concept of association and of laws of association, although since Hume it too was as a rule thought of as referring to the connections of the pure life of the soul, is only a naturalistic distortion of the corresponding genuine intentional concepts. Through phenomenology, which found access to the investigation of association very late, this concept receives a completely new face, an essentially new delimitation with new basic forms, to which, for example, sensuous configuration in coexistence and succession belongs. It is phenomenologically evident, but strange to one caught in tradition, that association is not a mere title for an empirical lawfulness of the complexion of data of a *soul*, something like an intra-psychic gravitation after the old picture, but a title, and moreover a most comprehensive one, for an intentional essential lawfulness of the constitution of the pure *ego*, a realm of the *innate* a priori, without which an *ego* as such is therefore unthinkable. Only through the phenomenology of genesis does the *ego* become intelligible as an infinite connection of synthetically belonging achievements, linked in the unity of universal genesis — in levels that must throughout fit the universal abiding form of temporality, because this form itself is built up in a constant, passive and completely universal genesis, which by essence embraces everything new. This structure of levels maintains itself in the developed *ego* as an abiding system of forms of apperception, and thereby of the constituted objectivities, among them an objective universe of fixed ontological structure; and this self-maintaining is itself only a form of genesis. In all this the fact in each case is irrational, but possible only in the system of forms of the a priori that belongs to it as an egological fact. Here it must not be overlooked that *fact* and its irrationality are themselves a structural concept in the system of the concrete a priori.

German:

> Das universale Prinzip der passiven Genesis für die Konstitution aller im aktiven Bilden letztlich vorgegebenen Gegenständlichkeiten trägt den Titel Assoziation. Es ist, wohlgemerkt, ein *Titel der Intentionalität*, als das in seinen Urgestalten deskriptiv aufweisbar, und in seinen intentionalen Leistungen unter Wesensgesetzen stehend, aus denen alle und jede passive Konstitution, sowohl diejenige der Erlebnisse als immanenter Zeitgegenstände als diejenige aller realen Naturgegenstände der objektiven raumzeitlichen Welt verständlich zu machen ist. **Assoziationisteintranszendental* - p h ä* nomenologischer Grundbegriff (wie auch in der psychologischen Parallele Grundbegriff einer rein intentionalen Psychologie). Der alte Begriff der Assoziation und der von Assoziationsgesetzen, obschon auch er seit Hume in der Regel auf die Zusammenhänge des reinen Seelenlebens bezogen gedacht war, ist nur eine naturalistische Verzerrung der entsprechenden echten intentionalen Begriffe. Durch die Phänomenologie, die sehr spät Zugänge zur Erforschung der Assoziation gefunden hat, erhält dieser Begriff ein völlig neues Gesicht, eine wesensmäßig neue Umgrenzung mit neuen Grundformen, wohin z.B. die sinnliche Konfiguration in Koexistenz und Sukzession gehört. Phänomenologisch evident, aber für den Traditionsbefangenen befremdlich ist, daß Assoziation nicht ein bloßer Titel für eine empirische Gesetzlichkeit der Komplexion von Daten einer *Seele* ist, nach dem alten Bilde so etwas wie eine innerseelische Gravitation, sondern ein, und zudem höchst umfassender, Titel für eine intentionale Wesensgesetzlichkeit der Konstitution des reinen *ego*, ein Reich des *eingeborenen* Apriori, ohne das also ein *ego* als solches undenkbar ist. Erst durch die Phänomenologie der Genesis wird das *ego* als ein unendlicher, in der Einheit universaler Genesis verknüpfter Zusammenhang von synthetisch zusammengehörigen Leistungen verständlich — in Stufen, die sich durchaus der universalen verharrenden Form der Zeitlichkeit fügen müssen, weil diese selbst in einer beständigen passiven und völlig universalen Genesis aufbaut, die wesensmäßig alles Neue mit umgreift. Dieser Stufenbau erhält sich im entwickelten *ego* als ein verharrendes Formensystem der Apperzeption und somit der konstituierten Gegenständlichkeiten, darunter eines objektiven Universums von fester ontologischer Struktur, und dieses Sich-erhalten ist selbst nur eine Form der Genesis. In all dem ist das jeweilige Faktum irrational, aber nur möglich in dem ihm als egologischen Faktum zugehörigen Formensystem des Apriori. Dabei ist aber nicht zu übersehen, daß *Faktum* und seine Irrationalität selbst ein Strukturbegriff im System des konkreten Apriori ist.

*Note on the text.* §39 is a single paragraph. The file keeps the print's short hyphenated lines here, and the quotation joins them. The sentence "Assoziation ist ein transzendental-phänomenologischer Grundbegriff" is emphasized, but the conversion has run it together and mixed bold, italic and a fragment of spaced type ("**Assoziationisteintranszendental* - p h ä* nomenologischer"). The emphasis is certain. Its exact form, and where it ends, cannot be told from the file, and the translation bolds the whole sentence. A stray marker "8" (a typescript signature) stands at the page break. The apparatus places the opening words on Hua I 113 (note 113,29 on "im aktiven Bilden", where the manuscript first had "den aktiven Gebilden" and Typescript C "dem aktiven Bilden"), and the rest falls on p. 114.

The first sentence keeps the old word and the second says what kind of word it is. "It is, be it noted, a *title of intentionality*" (*wohlgemerkt, ein* Titel der Intentionalität). "Be it noted" warns the reader off the sense the word brings. "Title" is the word Husserl uses for reason and unreason in §23. It marks a heading for a field of problems, not the name of a force or a faculty. Association is "of intentionality". It connects senses, as one appearance awakens another as belonging to it, and so it is "descriptively demonstrable in its primal forms". A causal law cannot be described in experience, only inferred from regularities. An intentional connection can be seen in the experience it shapes. The scope is total: "each and every passive constitution", of the stream's own experiences as much as of the objective world's things, is to be "made intelligible" from association's laws.

The polemic sentence has a concession inside it, and the concession sharpens the charge. The old concept is "only a naturalistic distortion" (*nur eine naturalistische Verzerrung*), "although since Hume it too was as a rule thought of as referring to the connections of the pure life of the soul". The obvious defence of the tradition is that association was always a psychological concept, about the mind and not about bodies. Husserl grants the premise and refuses the defence. Being about the soul did not keep the concept from being naturalistic, because it treated the soul's contents as nature treats its bodies, as data that combine by law. The next sentence names the model, and calls it a "picture": "something like an intra-psychic gravitation after the old picture" (*nach dem alten Bilde so etwas wie eine innerseelische Gravitation*). The tradition explained mental connection by analogy with the attraction of masses. "Complexion of data" (*Komplexion von Daten*) is §16's target again (row 13), the sensualism that builds consciousness from data and then adds Gestalt qualities to bind them. The reader who finds the new sense "strange" is one "caught in tradition" (*Traditionsbefangenen*), the condition the *epochē* was meant to suspend. The genitive "of a *soul*" is italicized. Association is not a law of a soul, a worldly entity, but of "the pure *ego*". The re-founded concept belongs to transcendental subjectivity, with its psychological parallel kept, as §§35 and 37 keep it.

The positive definition joins the vocabulary of rows 19 and 21. Association is "a realm of the *innate* a priori" (*eingeborenen*, italic). In §§3 and 10 the "innate" principles were Descartes', the axioms from which he hoped to infer the world (Hua I 49, 63). Here "innate" means what the ego cannot be without, an a priori of its genesis, not a stock of truths. The italic marks the word as borrowed and changed. The next sentences tie association to the two levels of §37. Universal genesis builds up the "abiding form of temporality" passively, and in the developed ego its levels "maintain themselves" as "an abiding system of forms of apperception", among them "an objective universe of fixed ontological structure". The world's stable ontology is a sediment of passive genesis, and "this self-maintaining is itself only a form of genesis". The paragraph ends by placing the fact. "The fact in each case is irrational", but possible only within "the system of forms of the a priori", and "*fact* and its irrationality are themselves a structural concept in the system of the concrete a priori". Contingency is not left outside the a priori. It is a place the a priori keeps for it, which is what §34's "mere example" had already implied.

The context explains why association has to be re-founded here. §38 has just shown that every active formation presupposes "a pregiving passivity" in which the thing is first given, and that this passive synthesis has its "*history*" announcing itself in it (Hua I 112–113 by the file's markers). §39 names the principle of that passivity. The concept is needed most in the Fifth Meditation. §51 introduces pairing as "a primal form of that passive synthesis which, as against the passive synthesis of *identification*, we call *association*" (Hua I 142). In §54 the other's body "does not pair in direct association" with my body's appearance Here but awakens how I would look There (Hua I 147; row 27). If association were a gravitation of data, the experience of others would be explained as a mechanical transfer, my sensations reproduced in another place. Because association is a "title of intentionality", pairing can be a transfer of sense. The other's body receives "analogically" the sense *living body*, and the analogy is apperceived without being inferred (§50; row 26).

The *Analyses Concerning Passive Synthesis*, lectures from the 1920s, introduce "associative synthesis" more cautiously. "What this association has to do with that of the naturalistic psychologists and of empirical psychology we leave in suspense for now" (*Analysen zur passiven Synthesis* §18, Hua XI 77, read in `husserl-hua-11-…`; the corpus caveat on that file concerns its Kant section, not this page). The lecture leaves open the relation that §39 settles in one sentence, "only a naturalistic distortion". The Meditations adopt the lectures' concept and add the verdict on the tradition and the name "since Hume". The verdict is stated, not argued, in §39. What supports it is the use the Fifth Meditation makes of the concept: association has to be able to carry the sense of another ego, and an intra-psychic gravitation could not do that.

## §41, Hua I 116–119: transcendental idealism as self-interpretation, and the countersense of transcendence

Reads [§41, Paragraphs 1–2](../commentaries/husserl/husserliana-1-cartesianische-meditationen-commentary.md#the-genuine-phenomenological-self-interpretation-of-the-ego-cogito-as-transcendental-idealism).

In §41 "transcendental idealism" is not a thesis about the world that phenomenology adopts. It is the name for what phenomenology turns out to be once it is carried through: the ego's systematic self-interpretation (*Selbstauslegung*), in which every sense of being, transcendence included, is shown as constituted in the ego. Nothing is argued for. The warrant for the idealism is the work done, and so "the *demonstration* of this *idealism* is phenomenology itself". The idealism gets its sense from what it opposes, and what it opposes is not a false answer but a countersense (*Widersinn*). The old problem of transcendence asks how I get out of the island of my consciousness. Asked by the natural human being, it already contains in its sense the validity of the world it asks about. Idealism does not give the realist's question the opposite answer. It shows that the question cannot be asked as it is posed. The passage also says, in sentences Husserl later bracketed and marked for rewriting, that this idealism is eidetic: it holds for every possible ego and every possible intersubjectivity. It does not settle how actual others are constituted in me, and that is why the Fifth Meditation follows.

> What has the transcendental self-reflection of phenomenology to say to this? Nothing other than that this whole problem is countersensical, a countersense into which Descartes himself had to fall because he missed the genuine sense of his transcendental *epochē* and of the reduction to the pure *ego*. But far cruder still, precisely through complete disregard of the Cartesian *epochē*, is the usual post-Cartesian cast of thought. We ask: who, then, is the I that can legitimately pose such *transcendental questions*? Can I do so as a natural human being, and can I as such ask seriously, and indeed transcendentally: how do I get out of my island of consciousness, how can what occurs in my consciousness as an experience of evidence gain objective significance? As soon as I apperceive myself as a natural human being, I have already apperceived the spatial world in advance, taken myself as in space, in which I therefore have an outside-me. Has not the validity of the world-apperception, then, already been presupposed in posing the question, entered into the sense of the question, whereas its answer was first to yield the right of objective validity at all? Evidently the conscious carrying-out of the phenomenological reduction is needed in order to gain that I and that conscious life from which transcendental questions, as questions of the possibility of transcendent cognition, are to be posed. But as soon as one, instead of fleetingly performing a phenomenological *epochē*, sets about uncovering one's entire field of consciousness in systematic self-reflection and as pure *ego*, one recognizes that everything that is for it is something constituting itself in it, and further that every kind of being, including every kind characterized as in any sense transcendent, has its particular constitution. Transcendence in every form is an immanent character of being, constituting itself within the ego. Every conceivable sense, every conceivable being, whether it is called immanent or transcendent, falls within the domain of transcendental subjectivity as the subjectivity that constitutes sense and being. To want to grasp the universe of true being as something that stands outside the universe of possible consciousness, possible cognition, possible evidence, the two related to each other merely externally by a rigid law, is nonsensical. By essence the two belong together, and what belongs together by essence is also concretely one, one in the single absolute concretion of transcendental subjectivity. If it is the universe of possible sense, then an outside is precisely nonsense. But even every nonsense is a mode of sense and has its nonsensicality in its being open to insight. This, however, does not hold for the merely factual ego and what is factually accessible to it as being for it, and, included therein, an open plurality of other egos and their constituting achievements, being for it. Spelled out more exactly: if, as is factually the case, other egos are transcendentally constituted in me, the transcendental ego, and if, constituted in turn by the transcendental intersubjectivity that thereby grows up constitutively for me, a general objective world is constituted, then everything said above holds not merely for my factual ego and for this factual intersubjectivity and world, which gain sense and validity of being in mine. The *phenomenological self-interpretation* carried out in my ego, the interpretation of all its constitutions and of the objectivities that are for it, necessarily took on the methodical form of an a priori one, a self-interpretation that orders the facts into the corresponding universe of pure (eidetic) possibilities. It therefore concerns my factual ego only insofar as that ego is one of the pure possibilities to be gained from it by freely thinking itself otherwise (fictively transforming itself). As eidetic, it therefore holds for the universe of these possibilities of mine as an ego in general, my possibilities of being otherwise in any way whatever; accordingly, then, also for every possible intersubjectivity related in correlative variation to these possibilities of mine [*tät.*] and, again, for the world to be thought as intersubjectively constituted in it. Genuine theory of knowledge is accordingly meaningful only as transcendental-phenomenological. Instead of dealing with countersensical inferences from a supposed immanence to a supposed transcendence, that of some allegedly in principle unknowable *things in themselves*, it has to do exclusively with the systematic clarification of the achievement of cognition, in which these must become intelligible through and through as intentional achievement. Precisely thereby every kind of being itself, real and ideal, becomes intelligible as a *formation* of transcendental subjectivity, constituted precisely in this achievement. This kind of intelligibility is the highest conceivable form of rationality. All perverse interpretations of being stem from naive blindness to the horizons that co-determine the sense of being, and to the corresponding tasks of uncovering implicit intentionality. If these are seen and grasped, there results as a consequence a universal phenomenology as a self-interpretation of the *ego*, carried out in constant evidence and at the same time in concretion. More precisely, and in the first place: as a self-interpretation in the pregnant sense, which shows systematically how the *ego* constitutes itself as a being in itself and for itself, of an essence of its own; and then, secondly, as a self-interpretation in the expanded sense, which shows from there how the *ego*, by virtue of this essence of its own, also constitutes in itself what is *other*, *objective*, and so everything whatever that ever has validity of being for it in the I as not-I.
>
