> Carried through in this systematic concretion, phenomenology is eo ipso *transcendental idealism*, though in a fundamentally new sense: not in that of a psychological idealism, not of an idealism that would derive a meaningful world from senseless sensuous data. Nor is it a Kantian idealism, which believes it can keep open, at least as a limit-concept, the possibility of a world of things in themselves. It is an idealism that is nothing more than a self-interpretation of my *ego* as subject of every possible cognition, consistently carried through in the form of systematic egological science, and indeed with respect to every sense of what is that is supposed to be able to have sense for me, the *ego*. This idealism is not a formation of playful argumentations, to be won as the prize of victory in dialectical strife with *realisms*. It is the interpretation of sense carried out in actual work on every type of being ever conceivable for me, the ego, and specifically on the transcendence of nature, of culture, of the world in general, which is actually pregiven to me through experience. But that says the same as: systematic uncovering of constituting intentionality itself. The *demonstration* of this *idealism* is therefore phenomenology itself. Only someone who misunderstands the deepest sense of intentional method, or that of the transcendental reduction, or even both, can want to separate phenomenology and transcendental idealism. Whoever commits the first misunderstanding has not even got so far as to have grasped the proper essence of a genuine intentional psychology (and of the intentional-psychological theory of knowledge included in it), nor its calling to become the foundation and core of a truly scientific psychology. But whoever mistakes the sense and achievement of the transcendental-phenomenological reduction still stands in transcendental psychologism. He confuses the parallel that arises from the essential possibility of a change of attitude, intentional psychology and transcendental phenomenology, and he falls into the countersense of a transcendental philosophy that remains standing on the natural ground.

German:

> Was hat die transzendentale Selbstbesinnung der Phänomenologie dazu zu sagen? Nichts anderes, als daß dieses ganze Problem widersinnig ist, ein Widersinn, in den Descartes selbst verfallen mußte, weil er den echten Sinn seiner transzendentalen *ἐποχή* und der Reduktion auf das reine *ego* verfehlte. Aber noch viel gröber, eben durch völlige Mißachtung der Cartesianischen *ἐποχή*, ist die gewöhnliche nachcartesianische Denkhaltung. Wir fragen, wer ist denn das Ich, das solche *transzendentalen Fragen* rechtmäßig stellen kann? Kann ich das als natürlicher Mensch, und kann ich als das ernstlich fragen, und zwar transzendental: Wie komme ich aus meiner Bewußtseinsinsel heraus, wie kann, was in meinem Bewußtsein als Evidenzerlebnis auftritt, objektive Bedeutung gewinnen? Sowie ich mich als natürlicher Mensch apperzipiere, habe ich ja schon im voraus die Raumwelt apperzipiert, mich als im Raume aufgefaßt, in dem ich also ein Außer-mir habe. Ist also nicht die Gültigkeit der Weltapperzeption schon in der Fragestellung vorausgesetzt worden, in den Sinn der Frage eingegangen, während doch ihre Beantwortung erst das Recht der objektiven Geltung überhaupt ergeben sollte? Es bedarf offenbar der bewußten Ausführung der phänomenologischen Reduktion, um dasjenige Ich und Bewußtseinsleben zu gewinnen, von dem transzendentale Fragen als Fragen der Möglichkeit transzendenter Erkenntnis zu stellen sind. Sowie man aber, statt flüchtig eine phänomenologische *ἐποχή* zu vollziehen, vielmehr darangeht, in systematischer Selbstbesinnung, und als reines *ego*, sein gesamtes Bewußtseinsfeld enthüllen zu wollen, erkennt man, daß alles für es Seiende sich in ihm selbst Konstituierendes ist, ferner daß jede Seinsart, darunter jede als in irgendeinem Sinne transzendent charakterisierte, ihre besondere Konstitution hat. Transzendenz in jeder Form ist ein immanenter, innerhalb des ego sich konstituierender Seinscharakter. Jeder erdenkliche Sinn, jedes erdenkliche Sein, ob es immanent oder transzendent heißt, fällt in den Bereich der transzendentalen Subjektivität als der Sinn und Sein konstituierenden. Das Universum wahren Seins fassen zu wollen als etwas, das außerhalb des Universums möglichen Bewußtseins, möglicher Erkenntnis, möglicher Evidenz steht, beides bloß äußerlich durch ein starres Gesetz aufeinander bezogen, ist unsinnig. Wesensmäßig gehört beides zusammen, und wesensmäßig Zusammengehöriges ist auch konkret eins, eins in der einzigen absoluten Konkretion der transzendentalen Subjektivität. Ist sie das Universum möglichen Sinnes, so ist ein Außerhalb dann eben Unsinn. Aber selbst jeder Unsinn ist ein Modus des Sinnes und hat seine Unsinnigkeit in der Einsehbarkeit. Das aber gilt nicht für das bloß faktische ego und das, was ihm faktisch zugänglich ist als für es Seiendes, und darin beschlossen eine als für es seiende offene Vielheit anderer ego’s und ihrer konstituierenden Leistungen. Genauer ausgeführt: Wenn in mir, dem transzendentalen ego, wie faktisch, andere ego’s transzendental konstituiert sind und, als von der mir damit konstitutiv erwachsenen transzendentalen Intersubjektivität ihrerseits konstituiert, eine allgemeine objektive Welt, so gilt alles vorhin Gesagte nicht bloß für mein faktisches ego und für diese faktische in der meinen Sinn und Seinsgeltung gewinnende Intersubjektivität und Welt. Die in meinem ego sich vollziehende *phänomenologische Selbstauslegung*, die aller seiner Konstitutionen und für es seienden Gegenständlichkeiten, nahm ja notwendig die methodische Gestalt einer apriorischen an, einer die Fakta in das entsprechende Universum purer (eidetischer) Möglichkeiten einordnenden Selbstauslegung. Sie betrifft also mein faktisches ego nur, insofern es eine der reinen Möglichkeiten ist, die aus ihm durch freies Umdenken (Umfingieren) seiner selbst zu gewinnen sind, sie gilt somit als eidetische für das Universum dieser meiner Möglichkeiten als ego überhaupt, meiner Möglichkeiten eines beliebigen Andersseins; demnach also auch für jede auf diese meine Möglichkeiten in korrelativer Abwandlung bezogene mögliche Intersubjektivität. tät und wieder in ihr als intersubjektiv konstituiert zu denkende Welt. Echte Erkenntnistheorie ist danach allein sinnvoll als transzendental-phänomenologische, die, statt mit widersinnigen Schlüssen von einer vermeinten Immanenz auf eine vermeinte Transzendenz, die irgendwelcher angeblich prinzipiell unerkennbarer *Dinge an sich*, es ausschließlich zu tun hat mit der systematischen Aufklärung der Erkenntnisleistung, in der sie durch und durch verständlich werden müssen als intentionale Leistung. Eben damit wird jede Art Seiendes selbst, reales und ideales, verständlich als eben in dieser Leistung konstituiertes *Gebilde* der transzendentalen Subjektivität. Diese Art Verständlichkeit ist die höchste erdenkliche Form der Rationalität. Alle verkehrten Seinsinterpretationen stammen aus der naiven Blindheit für die den Seinsinn mitbestimmenden Horizonte und für die zugehörigen Aufgaben der Enthüllung der impliziten Intentionalität. Werden sie erschaut und ergriffen, so ergibt sich als Konsequenz eine universale Phänomenologie als eine in steter Evidenz und dabei in Konkretion durchgeführte Selbstauslegung des *ego*. Genauer gesprochen, und fürs erste: als eine Selbstauslegung im prägnanten Sinne, die systematisch zeigt, wie das *ego* sich als in sich und für sich Seiendes eines eigenen Wesens konstituiert; und dann zweitens als eine Selbstauslegung im erweiterten Sinne, die von da aus zeigt, wie das *ego* in sich vermöge dieses Eigenwesens auch *Anderes*, *Objektives* konstituiert, und so überhaupt alles, was für es je im Ich als Nicht-Ich Seinsgeltung hat.
>
> In dieser systematischen Konkretion durchgeführt ist die Phänomenologie eo ipso *transzendentaler Idealismus*, obschon in einem grundwesentlich neuen Sinne; nicht in dem eines psychologischen Idealismus, nicht eines Idealismus, der aus sinnlosen sensuellen Daten eine sinnvolle Welt ableiten will. Nicht ist es ein Kantianischer Idealismus, der mindestens als Grenzbegriff die Möglichkeit einer Welt von Dingen an sich glaubt offen halten zu können — sondern ein Idealismus, der nichts weiter ist als in Form systematisch egologischer Wissenschaft konsequent durchgeführte Selbstauslegung meines *ego* als Subjektes jeder möglichen Erkenntnis, und zwar in Hinsicht auf jeden Sinn von Seiendem, mit dem es für mich, das *ego*, eben soll Sinn haben können. Dieser Idealismus ist nicht ein Gebilde spielerischer Argumentationen, im dialektischen Streit mit *Realismen* als Siegespreis zu gewinnen. Es ist die an jedem mir, dem ego, je erdenklichen Typus von Seiendem, und speziell an der (mir durch Erfahrung wirklich vorgegebenen) Transzendenz der Natur, der Kultur, der Welt überhaupt, in wirklicher Arbeit durchgeführte Sinnauslegung. Dasselbe aber sagt: systematische Enthüllung der konstituierenden Intentionalität selbst. Der *Erweis* dieses *Idealismus* ist also die Phänomenologie selbst. Nur wer den tiefsten Sinn der intentionalen Methode oder den der transzendentalen Reduktion oder gar beider mißversteht, kann Phänomenologie und transzendentalen Idealismus trennen wollen: wer das eine Mißverständnis begeht, ist nicht einmal so weit, das eigentliche Wesen einer echten intentionalen Psychologie (und darin beschlossenen einer intentional-psychologischen Erkenntnislehre) begriffen zu haben sowie ihren Beruf, das Grund- und Kernstück einer wahrhaft wissenschaftlichen Psychologie zu werden. Wer aber Sinn und Leistung der transzendental-phänomenologischen Reduktion verkennt, der steht noch im transzendentalen Psychologismus, er vermengt die aus der Wesensmöglichkeit der Einstellungsänderung hervorgehende Parallele: intentionale Psychologie und transzendentale Phänomenologie, er verfällt dem Widersinn einer Transzendentalphilosophie, die auf dem natürlichen Boden stehen bleibt.

*Note on the text.* The first sentences of §41 stand on one long line in the file, and the rest keeps the print's short hyphenated lines, which the quotation joins. The file sets the marker for Hua I 117 just after the § heading, but the apparatus puts §41's first sentence on p. 116 (note 116,14 falls on "seiner", in that sentence) and the sentence "Aber selbst jeder Unsinn…" on 117,16f, so p. 117 begins only near the end of the first long line. As in §37, the markers run ahead at the start of the section. At the break between pp. 117 and 118 the file reads "Intersubjektivi-" / "tät." / page marker / "tät und wieder in ihr…": the syllable is doubled and a full stop is set after the first copy. The apparatus counts "Welt." as 118,2 and treats the stretch "bis Welt." as one unit (117,22–118,2), so the sentence most likely runs on to "Welt." with nothing lost. The file cannot confirm this, so the translation marks the stray "[*tät.*]" and reads one sentence. "Seinsinn" (for *Seinssinn*, sense of being) and the plural "ego’s" are as in the file. The apparatus records many later marks in Husserl's copy, and the quotation gives the printed text: an insertion after "seiner" (116,14), the sentence "Aber selbst jeder Unsinn…" bracketed (117,16f), the next sentence, to "Genauer ausgeführt:", bracketed and struck (117,18–21), "Stil" [style] in the margin (117,19f), the stretch to "Welt." marked with wavy lines, closed with a bracket and annotated "Das muß anders behandelt werden" [This must be treated differently] (117,22–118,2), "nur" [only] struck in "mein faktisches ego nur" (117,33), wavy lines beside the countersensical inferences (118,4–8), three exclamation marks and an unclosed bracket before "Genauer gesprochen" (118,18–20), two large exclamation marks and a zero sign beside the two senses of self-interpretation (118,20–25), and insertions after "psychologischen Idealismus" (118,28f) and after "offen halten zu können" (118,33). The *Pariser Vorträge* print the definition of the new idealism in spaced type (Hua I 33). The Meditations file has no emphasis there, but spaced type survives in this file only in fragments, so nothing follows from its absence.

The first word the section uses against the old problem is not "false" but "countersensical" (*widersinnig*), and the next four sentences show what the word means. In §34 countersense was the opposite of the possible sense that eidetic law prescribes for every statement about the transcendental (Hua I 106). Here it takes a precise form. Husserl asks who poses the question, and the answer is the natural human being. Whoever apperceives himself as a human being "has already apperceived the spatial world in advance" and himself in it, "in which I therefore have an outside-me" (*ein Außer-mir*). The question "how do I get out of my island of consciousness" (*Bewußtseinsinsel*) uses that outside to describe the island. Something can be an island only in a sea. The validity of the world-apperception has therefore "entered into the sense of the question" (*in den Sinn der Frage eingegangen*), while the answer was supposed to establish that validity for the first time. A countersense in this sense is a question whose own sense presupposes what its answer is meant to secure. That is why Descartes "had to" (*mußte*) fall into it. Husserl's later insertion names what Descartes performed instead of the *epochē*: he missed "the genuine sense of his reduction to the indubitable, we meant to say of his transcendental *epochē*" (116,14). A reduction to the indubitable looks for a secure premise inside the island and then infers outward from it. §10 had already called Descartes "the father of the countersensical transcendental realism", and had added "(as cannot yet become visible here)" (Hua I 63). §41 is where it becomes visible.

The outside of the universe of possible consciousness gets a different word. To grasp true being as standing outside it is "nonsensical" (*unsinnig*), and "an outside is precisely nonsense" (*Unsinn*). The difference is deliberate, and the *Paris Lectures* show it was made in revision. There the outside was "a countersense" and "a nonsense" at once, and the inferences of traditional epistemology were "nonsensical" (*unsinnigen Schlüssen*, Hua I 32–33). The Meditations take "countersense" away from the outside and give it to the inferences: they speak of "countersensical inferences from a supposed immanence to a supposed transcendence" (*widersinnigen Schlüssen*). The revision sorts the two words. *Widersinn* belongs to questions, inferences and positions that have a sense which undoes itself. *Unsinn* belongs to the outside, which has no sense to undo. The next sentence closes off even that: "even every nonsense is a mode of sense and has its nonsensicality in its being open to insight" (*in der Einsehbarkeit*). That the outside is nonsense is itself seen, and seen inside the universe of sense, so there is no exit even through the word that names it. Husserl later bracketed this sentence (117,16f).

The sentences that follow carry the passage's most exposed claim, and they are the ones Husserl marked most heavily. As printed, "This, however, does not hold for the merely factual ego" contradicts the next sentence, which says that everything said "holds *not merely* for my factual ego". The *Paris Lectures* have the same first sentence and then simply extend the claim: "everything holds for every possible, conceivable ego and every conceivable being, and so for all conceivable worlds" (Hua I 33). The sense intended is clearly "not merely", and the word "merely" (*bloß*) sits on "factual" instead. The Meditations add what the lectures lack: the factual ego includes "an open plurality of other egos", and the eidetic claim covers "every possible intersubjectivity" and the world constituted in it. Husserl struck the faulty sentence, wrote "style" beside it, and marked the whole stretch "This must be treated differently". The claim itself is clear. The idealism is eidetic. Self-interpretation took "the methodical form of an a priori one" (§34's free variation, "fictively transforming itself", *Umfingieren*), so it concerns my factual ego "only insofar as" it is one pure possibility among others. The struck "only" (117,33) shows Husserl easing even this restriction. What the idealism does not yet have is the fact from which the variation starts: other egos are said to be "transcendentally constituted in me, as is factually the case" (*wie faktisch*), in a conditional clause, before anything has shown how.

The positive term is "self-interpretation" (*Selbstauslegung*), and the Meditations revise it from the lectures as well. The *Paris Lectures* define the new idealism as "self-interpretation of every sense of being" (*jedweden Seinssinnes*, Hua I 33). §41 makes it "self-interpretation of my *ego* as subject of every possible cognition, … with respect to every sense of what is". The ego is now what is interpreted, and the senses of being are the respect in which it is interpreted. The first paragraph splits the word in two. Self-interpretation "in the pregnant sense" shows how the ego constitutes itself "as a being of an essence of its own". Self-interpretation "in the expanded sense" shows how, "by virtue of this essence of its own", it also constitutes "what is *other*, *objective*", everything that has validity "in the I as not-I". This is the plan of the Fifth Meditation in one sentence: first the sphere of ownness (§44, row 25), then the other constituted on its basis. The exclamation marks and the zero sign Husserl later set beside the two senses (118,18–25) are not explained by the apparatus. They show that the sentence on which the Fifth Meditation's plan depends is among those he returned to.

The second paragraph turns on two nouns. The first is "formation" (*Gebilde*). Every kind of being, "real and ideal", has just become intelligible as a "*formation* of transcendental subjectivity", italicized. A few lines later, "this idealism is not a formation of playful argumentations" (*nicht ein Gebilde spielerischer Argumentationen*). Being is formed in the ego's achievement. The idealism is not formed in argument, and it is not "to be won as the prize of victory in dialectical strife with *realisms*". The plural and the italic make realism a family of positions on the natural ground, not one opponent. "Dialectical" names the method §41 rejects: settling the relation of consciousness and world by argument, without the work of description. The second noun is "demonstration" (*Erweis*, not *Beweis*, proof). The idealism is shown as it is carried out, and its demonstration is "phenomenology itself". The *Paris Lectures* had said "the carrying-out (*Durchführung*) of phenomenology itself" (Hua I 34). The Meditations drop "carrying-out", add "therefore" (*also*) and italicize both *Erweis* and *Idealismus*. The phrasing now identifies the demonstration with phenomenology outright, and "eo ipso" (by that very fact) at the head of the paragraph says the same. The inference is stronger in wording than in fact. The work it appeals to is "infinite" by §41's own later account, and the Meditations have only sketched it. Two paragraphs on, Husserl asks whether "the evidence gained" is sufficient, given that the existence of others for us is "very strange, we all feel it", and has not been made intelligible (Hua I 119–120). The demonstration is promised in the very paragraph that claims it.

The idealism is set against three others. Psychological idealism, in Husserl's later insertion, is "perhaps even one in the sense of sensualist psychologism" (118,28f). That is the target of §16 and §39, the derivation of a world from "senseless sensuous data" (rows 13 and 22). The file reads "Kantian idealism" as *Kantianischer*, where the lectures have *Kantischer* (Hua I 33). The change may point at the Kantians rather than at Kant, but the file cannot settle whether it is deliberate. The objection to Kant is the limit-concept of things in themselves. Husserl's later insertion widens it: such an idealism "would mean for the I an I in itself, mythically belonging to it, and Kantian transcendentalism would by no means pass over into the phenomenological one treated here if such doctrines were eradicated" (118,33). Removing the thing in itself would not make Kant a phenomenologist, because the difference lies in the method, not in one doctrine. The last sentences say what that method is. Whoever separates phenomenology from idealism misreads intentional method, or the reduction, or both, and the second misreading "still stands in transcendental psychologism". The paragraph ends where §41 began: such a reader "falls into the countersense of a transcendental philosophy that remains standing on the natural ground". In the first sentence countersense named a question. In the last it names a whole philosophy. The structure is the same: transcendental questions asked by someone who still takes himself as a human being in the world.

§40 has set up the problem in the natural attitude, with the ego talking "the natural I-talk". Husserl's copy carries two marks there. The clause that recognizes intentionality "following F. Brentano" is bracketed, and those three words are struck (115,16), and the "real" property that intentionality is said to be in a human being is struck with an exclamation mark in the margin (115,18). These marks point to the confusion §41's last sentence names, intentionality taken as a worldly property. After §41 the argument does not rest. §42 opens with the objection that the reduction makes me a *solus ipse* [sole self] and asks: "Have we not then done transcendental realism an injustice?" Realism, it concedes, "keeps its right in principle" insofar as it seeks "a way from the immanence of the ego to the transcendence of the Other" (Hua I 121). §41 dismissed the realisms in the plural. §42 gives one of them its due as an objection, and then refuses its method in §41's words: before engaging in "dialectical argumentations" and hypotheses that call themselves "'metaphysical'", "whose supposed possibility may turn out to be complete countersense", the concrete work must be done (Hua I 122). The file is damaged at the page break between the two sentences (Hua I 121/122, row 24), but neither quoted phrase falls in the gap. §62 closes the circle. Only the Fifth Meditation's analyses make "the full and proper sense of phenomenological-transcendental 'idealism'" intelligible. The word now stands in quotation marks, as it does in §41's title, and "this idealism turned out to be a monadology" (Hua I 176–177). The same paragraph uses §41's distinction outright: "in an *expanded* sense" the ego gains all transcendence "through *self-interpretation*" (Hua I 175). §64 keeps the countersense in its last appearance, excluding only "naive metaphysics operating with countersensical things in themselves" (Hua I 182).

The *Nachwort* to *Ideas I*, first published in 1930, says the same in different words and with a doubt that §41 does not express. Transcendental phenomenology, "even if no word about idealism is said, is in itself universal idealism, carried out as science", and "it demonstrates it" (*erweist ihn*) "through its own sense as transcendental science" (Hua V 152–153, read in `husserl-hua-5-…`:2313). The verb is §41's. The *Nachwort* also names the countersense on both sides: an idealism that knows only psychological subjectivity "is countersensical, is psychological idealism — the one that the equally countersensical realism combats", and their dispute is "unfruitful and unphilosophical", "moving on the natural ground" (Hua V 154, :2322). But the same text concedes that *Ideas I* might better have left "the final decision for transcendental idealism" open and shown only that thoughts "pressing towards an 'idealism'" must be thought through (Hua V 150–151, :2300–2304; the file loses text at the next page break, :2308–2311, after the passages used here). §41's "eo ipso" makes no such concession.

Rudolf Bernet's reading of Husserl's idealism separates a "broad" sense, in which every possibility needs a possible intuitive consciousness, from a "strict" sense, in which the actual existence of real things depends on the actual experience of embodied subjects in community (*Husserl's Transcendental Idealism Revisited*, pp. 18–19, `bernet-husserls-transcendental-idealism-revisited.md`:499–507). He finds the strict sense already in Husserl's 1913 revision of the Sixth Logical Investigation. §41, in its printed text, claims the broad sense explicitly: the idealism holds eidetically, for every possible ego and every possible intersubjectivity. The factual others enter only in the conditional "as is factually the case", and the sentences that tie the claim to "the merely factual ego" are the ones Husserl struck and marked for rewriting. Bernet's question is whether Husserl already held the strict idealism. In the Meditations that idealism is not yet asserted in §41. It is what the Fifth Meditation has to earn, and §62's "monadology" is the name it takes when it does. What §41 secures is the negative half: the problem of transcendence, asked from the island of consciousness, is a countersense, and the demonstration of the idealism that replaces it is the phenomenological work still to be done.

## §42, Hua I 121–122: the *solus ipse* objection and the refusal of dialectic

Reads [§42, Paragraph 1](../commentaries/husserl/husserliana-1-cartesianische-meditationen-commentary.md#exposition-of-the-problem-of-the-experience-of-others-in-contrast-to-the-objection-of-solipsism).

The objection of solipsism, as §42 states it, is not an attack from outside. It is the reduction's own doubt about itself. If everything I can know after the *epochē* is a unity constituted in my stream of experience, then the others I experience are such unities too, and I am "*solus ipse*" [the self alone]. Phenomenology could then not be what it claims to be, transcendental philosophy, able to solve the problems of the objective world. Husserl gives this doubt its strongest form by letting it reopen §41's verdict on transcendental realism. Then he declines to answer it by argument. What he offers in its place is a task: to show in which intentionalities the sense "other ego" forms itself in me and verifies itself as existing. The objection is answered by being turned into the programme of the Fifth Meditation, and §62 claims the answer only once that programme has been carried out.

> Let us tie our new meditations to an objection that may seem weighty. It concerns nothing less than the claim of transcendental phenomenology to be already transcendental philosophy, that is, to be able to solve the transcendental problems of the objective world in the form of a constitutive problematic and theory moving within the frame of the transcendentally reduced ego. If I, the meditating I, reduce myself through the phenomenological *epochē* to my absolute transcendental ego, have I not then become *solus ipse*, and do I not remain so as long as, under the title phenomenology, I carry on consistent self-interpretation? Would a phenomenology that wanted to solve problems of objective being, and to come forward already as philosophy, not then have to be branded as transcendental solipsism?
>
> Let us consider more closely. The transcendental reduction binds me to the stream of my pure conscious experiences and to the unities constituted through their actualities and potentialities. It now seems self-evident, surely, that such unities are inseparable from my ego and so belong to its concretion itself.
>
> But how then do matters stand with other egos, which are surely not mere representation and represented in me, synthetic unities of possible verification in me, but, according to their sense, precisely others? Have we then not done transcendental realism an injustice? It may lack a phenomenological grounding, but in principle it keeps its right insofar as it seeks a way from the immanence of the ego to the transcendence of the other. Can we as phenomenologists, following this up, say anything other than that nature and world in general, constituted immanently in the ego, has behind it first of all the world itself existing *in itself*, to which the way must first be sought; and so say: even the question of the possibility of actually transcendent cognition, above all of the possibility of how I get from my absolute ego to other egos, which as others are not actually in me but only conscious in me, cannot be posed purely phenomenologically? Is it not self-evident from the start that my transcendental field of cognition does not reach beyond my transcendental sphere of experience and what is synthetically included in it — self-evident that everything together is designated and exhausted by my own transcendental ego?
>
> Yet perhaps not everything is in order in such thoughts. Before one decides for them and for the "self-evidences" made use of in them, and even goes so far as to engage in dialectical argumentations and in hypotheses that call themselves "metaphysical", whose supposed possibility may turn out to be complete countersense, it might after all be more fitting first to attack systematically, and carry through in concrete work, the task of phenomenological interpretation that announces itself here with the *alter ego*. We must surely gain insight into the explicit and implicit intentionality in which, on the ground of our transcendental ego, the *alter ego* announces and verifies itself; how, in which intentionalities, in which syntheses, in which motivations the sense *other ego* forms itself in me and, under the titles of concordant experience of others, verifies itself as existing, and in its way even as itself there. These experiences and their achievements are indeed transcendental facts of my phenomenological sphere — from where else than by questioning them can I interpret on all sides the sense of existing others?

German:

> Knüpfen wir unsere neuen Meditationen an einen, wie es scheinen möchte, schwerwiegenden Einwand. Nichts Geringeres betrifft er als den Anspruch der transzendentalen Phänomenologie, schon Transzendentalphilosophie zu sein, also in Form einer im Rahmen des transcendental reduzierten ego sich bewegenden konstitutiven Problematik und Theorie die transzendentalen Probleme der objektiven Welt lösen zu können. Wenn ich, das mediierende Ich, mich durch die phänomenologische *ἐποχή* auf mein absolutes transzendentales ego reduziere, bin ich dann nicht zum *solus ipse* geworden, und bleibe ich es nicht, solange ich unter dem Titel Phänomenologie konsequente Selbstauslegung betreibe? Wäre also eine Phänomenologie, die Probleme objektiven Seins lösen und schon als Philosophie auftreten wollte, nicht als transzendentaler Solipsismus zu brandmarken?
>
> Überlegen wir näher. Die transzendentale Reduktion bindet mich an den Strom meiner reinen Bewußtseinserlebnisse und an die durch ihre Aktualitäten und Potentialitäten konstituierten Einheiten. Es scheint nun doch selbstverständlich, daß solche Einheiten von meinem ego unabtrennbar sind und somit zu seiner Konkretion selbst gehören.
>
> Aber wie steht es dann mit anderen ego's, die doch nicht bloße Vorstellung und Vorgestelltes in mir sind, synthetische Einheiten möglicher Bewährung im mir, sondern sinngemäß eben Andere. Haben wir also dem transzendentalen Realismus nicht Unrecht getan? Es mag ihm an phänomenologischer Grundlegung fehlen, aber im Prinzipiellen behält er Recht insofern, als er einen Weg von der Immanenz des ego zur Transzendenz des Anderen. [page break] dern sucht. Können wir als Phänomenologen anders als dem nachgehend sagen, die im ego immanent konstituierte Natur und Welt überhaupt habe hinter sich allererst die *an sich* seiende Welt selbst, zu der eben der Weg erst zu suchen sei; und somit sagen: Schon die Frage der Möglichkeit wirklich transzendenter Erkenntnis, vor allem der Möglichkeit, wie ich aus meinem absoluten ego zu anderen ego's komme, die doch als andere nicht wirklich in mir, sondern in mir nur bewußte sind, sei rein phänomenologisch nicht zu stellen. Ist es nicht von vornherein selbstverständlich, daß mein transzendentales Erkenntnisfeld über meine transzendentale Erfahrungssphäre und das in ihr synthetisch Beschlossene nicht hinausreicht — selbstverständlich, daß alles in eins durch mein eigenes transzendentales ego bezeichnet und erschöpft ist?
>
> Indessen vielleicht ist doch in solchen Gedanken nicht alles in Ordnung. Ehe man sich für sie und die in ihnen verwerteten „Selbstverständlichkeiten“ entscheidet und nun gar sich in dialektische Argumentationen und in „metaphysisch“ sich nennende Hypothesen einläßt, deren vermeinte Möglichkeit sich vielleicht als vollkommener Widersinn herausstellt, dürfte es doch angemessener sein, zunächst die sich hier mit dem *alter ego* anzeigende Aufgabe der phänomenologischen Auslegung in konkreter Arbeit systematisch anzugreifen und durchzuführen. Wir müssen uns doch Einblick verschaffen in die explizite und implizite Intentionalität, der sich auf dem Boden unseres transzendentalen ego das *alter ego* bekundet und bewährt, wie, in welchen Intentionalitäten, in welchen Synthesen, in welchen Motivationen der Sinn *anderes ego* sich in mir gestaltet und unter den Titeln einstimmiger Fremderfahrung sich als seiend, und in seiner Weise sogar als selbst da sich bewährt. Diese Erfahrungen und ihre Leistungen sind ja transzendentale Tatsachen meiner phänomenologischen Sphäre — kann ich wo anders her als durch ihre Befragung den Sinn seiender Anderer allseitig auslegen?

*Note on the text.* The file garbles the break between Hua I 121 and 122. The third paragraph ends on p. 121 with "zur Transzendenz des Anderen." [to the transcendence of the other.], and p. 122 opens with "dern sucht." The likeliest cause is the doubling the file shows at §41's page break (Hua I 117–118): the print had the word divided across the pages, perhaps as "An-dern", and the conversion completed it and closed the sentence on the first page while keeping the second half on the next. Nothing else seems to be lost, and the sense is clear: realism "seeks a way" to the other. The quotation gives what the file has, with the break marked, and the English renders the evident sentence. Three smaller oddities are as in the file: "mediierende" for *meditierende* [meditating], "transcendental" for *transzendental*, and "im mir" for *in mir* [in me]. The file also has "der sich" where the sense needs "in der sich" [in which]; the first version of the text (Hua XV, below) reads "in der sich", and the English follows the sense. The apparatus records later marks in Husserl's copy that the printed text does not show: "Scheint? Ist." [Seems? Is.] in the margin at 121,22; an insertion after "überhaupt" [in general] at 122,3, "sei nur meine Vorstellung" [is only my representation]; and, beside the last paragraph (122,25ff.), the marginal note "Die gefährliche Ich-Rede, mein-Rede! Das Sprachliche erörtern" [The dangerous I-talk, my-talk! Discuss the linguistic], with "unseres" [our], "in mir" [in me] and "meiner" [my] struck at 122,25, 122,28 and 122,31.

The objection is staged in the first person and in questions. "Have I not then become *solus ipse*, and do I not remain so" (*bin ich dann nicht … geworden, und bleibe ich es nicht*) moves from a result to a condition that lasts. The reduction does not merely begin with the ego. As long as phenomenology is "consistent self-interpretation" (*konsequente Selbstauslegung*), §41's own title for it, I seem to stay alone. That is what makes the objection weighty. The very term in which §41 had just claimed transcendental idealism now looks like the proof of solipsism. The verb of the last sentence is a verb of public judgement. To "brand" (*brandmarken*) is to mark something with a name it cannot shake off. The worry is no longer, as in §13 (Hua I 69; row 10), that solipsism is a methodical substage to be passed through. It is that phenomenology deserves the name and must give up its claim "to come forward already as philosophy" (*schon als Philosophie auftreten*). The "already" (*schon*), used twice, sets the stakes: the claim that the egological work, as it stands, is philosophy and not a preparation for it.

The second and third paragraphs give the objection its argument, and in doing so turn back on §41. The reduction "binds" me to my stream and its unities. That the unities are inseparable from my ego "seems self-evident, surely" (*Es scheint nun doch selbstverständlich*). Then comes the step the commentary's block leaves out: "Have we then not done transcendental realism an injustice?" (*Haben wir also dem transzendentalen Realismus nicht Unrecht getan?*). §10 had called Descartes "the father of countersensical transcendental realism" (*widersinnigen transzendentalen Realismus*, Hua I 63), and §41 had declared the problem of transcendence a countersense. Here the objection grants realism its "right" (*Recht*) "in principle", with only a missing phenomenological grounding against it: it at least "seeks a way" from immanence to the transcendence of the other. The next question pushes the concession into a dilemma. If we follow realism, we must say that the world constituted in me has the world "*in itself*" (*an sich*, italic) behind it, and so admit that phenomenology cannot even pose the question of how I get to other egos. If we refuse, we must say that my field of cognition "is designated and exhausted" (*bezeichnet und erschöpft*) by my own ego. Every step of the dilemma is a question, and the claims inside it are in the subjunctive of reported speech: the question "cannot be posed" (*sei … nicht zu stellen*). Husserl voices the objection without asserting it. The decisive phrase is the contrast "not actually in me, but only conscious in me" (*nicht wirklich in mir, sondern in mir nur bewußte*). The objection treats "in me" as a place, and asks how anything could leave it.

The fourth paragraph refuses the dilemma by refusing its terms, and the wording carries the refusal. "Perhaps not everything is in order" is the mildest possible opening. The force sits in the quotation marks. The "self-evidences" (*„Selbstverständlichkeiten“*) are the three uses of "self-evident" in the paragraphs before; the scare quotes withdraw them. The "metaphysical" (*„metaphysisch“*) is what the hypotheses "call themselves". Their "supposed possibility" may prove "complete countersense" (*vollkommener Widersinn*). That is §41's word, and it matters that it is not "nonsense" (*Unsinn*). A countersense is a question whose sense contradicts itself, and the objection's "in me" is such a sense: it takes the ego for a container while asking about the experience that constitutes the container's outside. Against "dialectical argumentations" the paragraph sets "concrete work". The plan is a series of interrogatives, "how, in which intentionalities, in which syntheses, in which motivations", and the object of the work is a sense, "the sense *other ego*" (*der Sinn* anderes ego, italic), which "forms itself in me" and "verifies itself as existing". The verbs are reflexive. The other "announces and verifies itself" (*bekundet und bewährt*): the experience of others is not inferred from my side but has its own mode of verification, even "as itself there". The last question turns the objection's premise around. That the experiences of others are "transcendental facts of my phenomenological sphere" was the ground of the solipsism worry. It becomes the reason why they can be questioned at all.

Here the weakness lies in the wording of the answer rather than of the objection. The programme still speaks of "*our* transcendental ego", of the sense forming itself "*in me*", of "*my* phenomenological sphere", the very phrases the objection relied on. Husserl saw this later. In his copy he wrote beside the paragraph "The dangerous I-talk, my-talk! Discuss the linguistic", and struck exactly those three words. In §40 the "I-talk" of the beginning had been "the natural I-talk" (*die natürliche Ich-Rede*, Hua I 115). The marginal note admits that the transcendental use of "I", "my" and "in me" has not been separated from the natural one, and that the objection lives from that confusion. The other marks point the same way. "Seems? Is." most likely answers the "seems self-evident" at 121,22, and so proposes that the inseparability of the unities be granted, not merely voiced. The insertion at 122,3, "is only my representation", sharpens the objection's first horn. Husserl's revisions made the objection stronger and the answer more careful about its words.

The context shows what the refusal commits the Meditation to. §43 takes as its "transcendental clue" the other as given, "purely as correlate of my *cogito*" (Hua I 122–123). It finds there the other as a psychophysical object in the world and as a subject for that same world, and the world as "*intersubjective*", "there for everyone". It concludes that the theory of the experience of others, "so-called *empathy*" (*Einfühlung*), co-founds a transcendental theory of the objective world (Hua I 123–124). §44 then carries out on purpose the abstraction the objection feared, the reduction to the sphere of ownness (row 25). The objection returns in §62 (Hua I 174–177; row 30) in its first form, the claim to be transcendental philosophy, with a new charge added: the step to others is possible only through "an unadmitted metaphysics, a secret taking-over of Leibnizian traditions". There it "dissolves in its groundlessness". §62 also names the source of the seeming. At the beginning the ego is grasped with "a wholly undetermined horizon", so "involuntarily I take myself, the ego, for a *solus ipse*". What §42 voiced "as it may seem" (*wie es scheinen möchte*) becomes in §62 "the semblance of a solipsism" (*der Schein eines Solipsismus*), and "metaphysical construction" is refused "in the sharpest contrast", with the concession of "deliberate echoes of Leibniz's metaphysics" (Hua I 176–177).

Two earlier versions show what the Meditations added. In the *Paris Lectures* the objection is "the only really disquieting misgiving", and phenomenology would be "a pure, though transcendental-phenomenological, solipsism" (PV, Hua I 34–35). The first version of the Fifth Meditation, written in spring 1929 (*Zur Phänomenologie der Intersubjektivität III*, Text Nr. 1, §1, Hua XV 3–4, read in `husserl-hua-15-…`), already has the *solus ipse* question, the branding and the turn to concrete work, nearly word for word. It lacks the whole concession to transcendental realism. And it states the dilemma's result flatly: the questions of transcendent cognition "are not to be posed purely phenomenologically" (*sind rein phänomenologisch nicht zu stellen*), and "it is self-evident from the start" (*Es ist von vornherein selbstverständlich*) that my field does not reach beyond my sphere. The Meditations turn both assertions into questions and add realism's claim to be in the right. They also replace "presuppositions … whose self-evidence" (*Voraussetzungen … deren Selbstverständlichkeit*) with "hypotheses … whose supposed possibility" (*Hypothesen … deren vermeinte Möglichkeit*). The revision sharpens the objection by giving it realism's support, and it makes the objection's voice clearly not Husserl's own. That is the understanding §42 establishes. The solipsism objection is what the reduction looks like before its sense of "in me" has been interpreted. The Meditation does not argue it away. It undertakes to show that "the sense *other ego*" has its own verification, and only §62 claims that the undertaking has succeeded.

## §44, Hua I 127–128: the living body as the one body I govern

Reads [§44, Paragraph 1](../commentaries/husserl/husserliana-1-cartesianische-meditationen-commentary.md#reduction-of-transcendental-experience-to-the-sphere-of-ownness).

In the sphere of ownness, the living body (*Leib*) is the one body among bodies (*Körper*) in which I govern. Husserl defines it before any other ego or any objective nature is in play, by what I do in it and can do with it: it is where I place my fields of sensation, where I "govern and act" immediately, through organs whose movements run in an "I do" and stand under an "I can", and it can perceive and handle itself. The definition is first-personal and practical, not a property found in an object. That is what the Fifth Meditation needs. The experience of others will rest on a body over there receiving "the sense *living body*" from mine, and that transfer needs an original that is given from within, in its uniqueness, before anything has made it one body among others.

> Let us consider more closely the result of our abstraction, that is, what it leaves us. From the phenomenon of the world, the world appearing with objective sense, an under-stratum separates itself off as **ownness** "*nature*", which must remain well distinguished from mere nature simply, that is, the nature that becomes the theme of the natural scientist. This too arises by abstraction, namely from everything psychic and from the predicates of the objective world that spring from persons. But what is gained in this abstraction of the natural scientist is a stratum belonging to the objective world itself (in transcendental attitude, to the objective sense "objective world"), hence itself objective, just as that from which it abstracts is in turn objective (objective psychic being, objective cultural predicates and so on). But in our abstraction the sense "objective" vanishes altogether, the sense that belongs to everything worldly as intersubjectively constituted, as experienceable for everyone and so on. Thus to my ownness, as purified of all sense of foreign subjectivity, there belongs a sense *mere nature* that has lost this *for-everyone* too, and so must by no means be taken for an abstract stratum of the world itself or of its sense. Among the bodies, properly taken, of this *nature* I then find, in unique distinction, **my living body**, namely as the only one that is not mere body but precisely **living body**: the only object within my abstract world-stratum to which, as experience has it, I ascribe fields of sensation, although in different manners of belonging (field of touch sensations, field of warmth and cold, and so on), the only one *in which I immediately hold sway and govern*, and govern in particular in each of its *organs*. I perceive, touching kinaesthetically with the hands, likewise seeing with the eyes, and so on, and can at any time perceive so, these kinaestheses of the organs running in the *I do* and standing under my *I can*; further, setting these kinaestheses in play, I can push, shove and so on, and thereby *act* bodily, immediately and then mediately. Perceptually active, I experience (or can experience) all nature, among it my own bodiliness, which is therefore in this referred back to itself. This becomes possible in that I *can* in each case perceive the one hand *by means of* the other, an eye by means of a hand, and so on, where the functioning organ must become object and the object functioning organ. And likewise for the generally possible original handling of *nature* and of bodiliness itself through bodiliness, which is therefore also practically referred to itself.

German:

> Betrachten wir das Ergebnis unserer Abstraktion näher, also das, was sie uns übrig läßt. Es scheidet sich am Phänomen der Welt, der mit objektivem Sinn erscheinenden, eine Unterschicht ab als **eigenheitliche** „*Natur*“, die wohl unterschieden bleiben muß von bloßer Natur schlechthin, also derjenigen, die das Thema des Naturforschers wird. Diese erwächst zwar auch durch Abstraktion, nämlich von allem Psychischen und von den personal entsprungenen Prädikaten der objektiven Welt. Aber was in dieser Abstraktion des Naturforschers gewonnen wird, ist eine zur objektiven Welt selbst (in transzendentaler Einstellung zum gegenständlichen Sinn „objektive Welt“) gehörige, also selbst objektive Schicht, wie denn das, wovon abstrahiert wird, seinerseits Objektives ist (objektives Psychisches, objektive Kulturprädikate usw.). Aber in unserer Abstraktion verschwindet ja der Sinn „objektiv“ ganz und gar, der allem Weltlichen zugehört als intersubjektiv Konstituiertem, als einem für jedermann Erfahrbaren usw. So gehört zu meiner Eigenheit als von allem Sinn fremder Subjektivität gereinigter ein Sinn *bloße Natur*, der eben auch dieses *Für-jedermann* verloren hat, also keineswegs für eine abstraktive Schicht der Welt selbst bzw. ihres Sinnes genommen werden darf. Unter den eigentlich gefaßten Körpern dieser *Natur* finde ich dann in einziger Auszeichnung **meinen Leib**, nämlich als den einzigen, der nicht bloßer Körper ist, sondern eben **Leib**, das einzige Objekt innerhalb meiner abstraktiven Weltschicht, dem ich erfahrungsgemäß Empfindungsfelder zurechne, obschon in verschiedenen Zugehörigkeitsweisen (Tastempfindungsfeld, Wärme-Kälte-Feld usw.), das einzige, *in dem ich unmittelbar schalte und walte*, und insonderheit walte in jedem seiner *Organe*. Ich nehme, mit den Händen kinästhetisch tastend, mit den Augen ebenso sehend usw., wahr und kann jederzeit so wahrnehmen, wobei diese Kinästhesen der Organe im *Ich tue* verlaufen und meinem *Ich kann* unterstehen; ferner kann ich, diese Kinästhesen ins Spiel setzend, stoßen, schieben usw. und dadurch unmittelbar und dann mittelbar leiblich *handeln*. Wahrnehmend tätig erfahre ich (oder kann ich erfahren) alle Natur, darunter die eigene Leiblichkeit, die darin also auf sich selbst zurückbezogen ist. Das wird dadurch möglich, daß ich jeweils *mittelst* der einen Hand die andre, mittelst einer Hand ein Auge usw. wahrnehmen *kann*, wobei fungierendes Organ zum Objekt und Objekt zum fungierenden Organ werden muß. Und ebenso für das allgemein mögliche ursprüngliche Behandeln der *Natur* und der Leiblichkeit selbst durch die Leiblichkeit, die also auch praktisch auf sich selbst bezogen ist.

*Note on the text.* The paragraph begins in the stretch where the file keeps the print's short hyphenated lines (Hua I 127), and the quotation joins them; from the page break to Hua I 128 it is a single long line. The apparatus has no note on these lines. "Unter den eigentlich gefaßten Körpern" is as in the file: *eigentlich* [properly, in the proper sense] occurs here and twice in the next paragraph ("meines eigentlich reduzierten Leibes", "eigentlich reduziere"), where the section otherwise says *eigenheitlich* [in the manner of ownness]. The file cannot show whether the print has the same word, and the reading does not rest on it.

The paragraph reaches the living body by subtraction from two other natures. The ownness "nature" (**eigenheitliche** „*Natur*“, bold adjective, the noun in quotation marks) is set against the natural scientist's "mere nature simply". Both arise by abstraction, and the paragraph insists on the difference. The scientist abstracts from the psychic and the cultural but keeps the sense "objective". His nature is still a stratum of the world for everyone, as the psychic and cultural he sets aside are objective too. The ownness abstraction removes that sense itself, "the *for-everyone*" (*Für-jedermann*). The quotation marks on "nature" say that the word is kept for something that is not nature in its usual sense, a world-layer that is mine but not yet "the world". A later paragraph of §44 says so of all its reduced terms: "therefore the constant quotation marks" (*darum die beständigen Anführungszeichen*, Hua I 129). The distinction matters for the body. If the ownness sphere were the scientist's nature, my body would be found there as one physical thing among others, and the living body would be a second layer on top of it, to be added afterwards. Because the objective sense has gone, the bodies of this "nature" are not yet objective bodies, and the living body is found among them first of all as mine.

The definition proceeds through one word, three times. My living body is found "in unique distinction" (*in einziger Auszeichnung*), "as the only one" (*den einzigen*) that is not mere body, "the only object" (*das einzige Objekt*) to which I ascribe fields of sensation, "the only one" (*das einzige*) in which I govern. The uniqueness defines the living body, but it is also the difficulty that §§50–54 must work through. If the living body is by definition the only one, a second one is a contradiction in terms. §51 asks the question plainly: "What makes the living body a foreign one, and not a second own living body?" (Hua I 143). The pair *Leib*/*Körper* carries this in a way English cannot. "Body" has to translate both, so the English needs "living body" for *Leib*, and the bare contrast "not mere body but precisely living body" (*nicht bloßer Körper … sondern eben Leib*, both nouns bold in the file) becomes a phrase. In German the two words name the same thing from two sides. My *Leib* is also a *Körper*, as the compound *Leibkörper* says later (Hua I 143, 146). The contrast is not between two objects but between a body as merely there and the same body as the one I live in.

The marks of the living body that follow are given in an order that moves from having to doing. Fields of sensation are "ascribed" (*zurechne*) "as experience has it", in "different manners of belonging", touch here, warmth and cold there. Then comes the idiom that carries the definition: the living body is the only one "*in which I immediately hold sway and govern*" (*in dem ich unmittelbar schalte und walte*, italic). *Schalten und walten* is everyday German for having free disposal of something, as a householder disposes of his house. Husserl takes the second verb and makes it a term: "and govern in particular in each of its *organs*". The governing is spelled out in kinaesthesis. The movements of the organs "run in the *I do*" and "stand under my *I can*" (*im* Ich tue *verlaufen und meinem* Ich kann *unterstehen*, both italic). The "I can" is §27's word for the in-itself (row 17) and returns here as the body's capacity. My body is the field of what I can always do again. Then the body becomes reflexive: through it I can experience "all nature, among it my own bodiliness". One hand perceives the other, "where the functioning organ must become object and the object functioning organ". The "must" (*muß*) states a condition, not a fact. If the body is to be perceived by itself, it has to occupy both places in turn. Here the inference is thinner than the wording. Husserl says that "all nature, among it my own bodiliness" is experienced "perceptually active". But the examples are touch, one hand on the other or a hand on an eye. They do not show that my body is given to me as an outer thing is, and §54 will not claim it. There the body of the other awakens not how my body looks to me here, but how it would look "*if I were there*" (*wenn ich dort wäre*, Hua I 147; row 27).

The context shows why the definition has to come here. §43 took as its clue others experienced "as psychically governing in the natural bodies belonging to them" (*in den ihnen zugehörigen Naturleibern psychisch waltende*, Hua I 123). That governing (*Walten*) was only read off the noematic phenomenon. §44 gives the word its original in my own case. After §44 it runs through the Meditation. In §50 the ego implied in "other" is "I myself", "as *personal* I immediately governing in my, the only, living body" (*unmittelbar waltend in meinem, dem einzigen Leib*, Hua I 140). In §52 the "foreign governing I" is given together with the foreign living body (Hua I 144). In §53 the other's living body is apperceived as constituted in its own monad "in the mode of the absolute *Here*, precisely as functional centre for its governing" (*als Funktionszentrum für sein Walten*, Hua I 146). The term the clue borrowed from everyday experience is here made a description of one's own case, and that case is the original from which the sense *living body* is transferred: in §54 the body there "receives analogically from mine the sense *living body*" (Hua I 147). §17 had already mentioned the "absolute *Here*" "in the co-appearing own living body" (Hua I 78). §44 is the place where that body, until then only a point of orientation, is defined.

*Ideas II* defines the living body by the same contrast, in the naturalistic attitude and as a property of a thing. There the living body is "organ of the will", "the only object which, for the will of my pure I, is immediately spontaneously movable", and a means to move other things which "my immediately spontaneously moved hand pushes, grasps, lifts" (*Ideen II* §38, Hua IV 151–152, read in `husserl-hua-4-…`). The vocabulary of the Meditations is close: "only", "immediately", "push, shove". But in *Ideas II* the living body is distinguished "from all material things", which are already given as objective, and the will is that of "my pure I" without the kinaesthetic "I do" and "I can". In §44 the living body is defined in a sphere from which the objective sense, and with it the material thing of the natural scientist, has been removed. What is unique about the living body is no longer a feature of one thing among things. It is the first articulation of my ownness itself, the place where governing happens. The Fifth Meditation needs exactly that. The sense *living body* has to be mine before it is anyone's, so that the experience of others can transfer it, and uniqueness has to belong to it by definition, so that the transfer can never make the other a second me.

## §50, Hua I 139–141: appresentation, an apperception and not an inference

Reads [§50, Paragraph 1](../commentaries/husserl/husserliana-1-cartesianische-meditationen-commentary.md#the-mediate-intentionality-of-the-experience-of-others-as-appresentation-analogical-apperception).

The other is given to me through appresentation: a making co-present that rides on something actually presented, the body over there, and that can never itself become presentation. Husserl's claim in §50 is that this appresentation is an apperception and not an inference. The body over there is apprehended as a living body by a transfer of sense from my own living body. The similarity of the two bodies motivates the transfer, but it is not a premise from which the other's inner life is concluded. Apperception is "no inference, no act of thought". It is the way every pregiven object is seen, as of a type whose sense was once instituted and is now carried over to the new case. The term that makes the claim work is "primal instituting" (*Urstiftung*). It ties the experience of others to the genesis of all sense in §§38–39, and the next section has to qualify it at once.

> Experience is original consciousness, and in fact in the case of the experience of a human being we say in general that the other stands there before us himself, *in person*. On the other hand, this being-in-person does not prevent us from conceding without further ado that what comes to original givenness here is not, properly speaking, the other I himself, not his experiences, his appearances themselves, nothing of what belongs to his own essence itself. Were that the case, were what belongs to the other's own essence accessible in a direct way, it would be merely a moment of my own essence, and in the end he himself and I myself would be one and the same. It would be similar with his living body if it were nothing other than the *body* that is a unity constituting itself purely in my actual and possible experiences, belonging to my primordial sphere as a formation exclusively of my *sensibility*. A certain *mediacy of intentionality* must be present here, and indeed one running out from the under-stratum of the *primordial world*, which in any case constantly lies at the basis, an intentionality that makes representable a *co-there* which is nevertheless not itself there and can never become an itself-there. It is therefore a matter of a kind of *making co-present*, a kind of *appresentation*.
>
> Such an appresentation is already present in outer experience, insofar as the properly seen front side of a thing always and necessarily appresents a thingly back side and prescribes for it a more or less determinate content. On the other hand, it cannot be precisely this kind of appresentation, the kind that already co-constitutes primordial nature, since to it belongs the possibility of verification through a corresponding fulfilling presentation (the back side becomes the front side), whereas for the appresentation that is to lead into another original sphere this must be excluded a priori. How can the appresentation of another sphere, and with it the sense *other*, be motivated in mine, and indeed as experience, as the very word appresentation (making-conscious-as-co-present) indicates? An arbitrary presentification cannot do it. It can do so only in interweaving with a presentation, a proper self-giving; and only as demanded by it can it have the character of appresentation, much as in the experience of things perceptual being-there motivates co-being-there.
>
> The under-ground of proper perception is offered to us by the continuously ongoing perception of the primordially reduced world, ordered into the general frame of the ego's constant self-perception, in the articulation we described earlier. The question now is what must come into consideration in this respect and how the motivation runs, how the quite complicated intentional achievement of the appresentation that factually comes about is to be disclosed.
>
> A first guide can be offered us by the verbal sense *other*: other I. *Alter* says *alter ego*, and the ego implied here is I myself, constituted within my primordial ownness, and indeed in uniqueness, as psychophysical unity (as primordinal human being), as *personal* I immediately governing in my, the only, living body, immediately also acting into the primordial surrounding world; for the rest, subject of a concrete intentional life, of a psychic sphere related to itself and to the *world*. All this, and indeed in the typification that grows up in experienced life, with the familiar forms of course and complexion, is at our disposal. Through what intentionalities, themselves highly complicated, it has constituted itself, we have of course not investigated. That forms a stratum of great investigations of its own, into which we have not gone and could not go.
>
> Suppose now that another human being enters our field of perception. Primordinally reduced, that means: in the perceptual field of my primordial nature a body appears which, as primordinal, is of course merely a determining piece of myself (*immanent transcendence*). Since in this nature and world my living body is the only body that is and can be originally constituted as living body (functioning organ), the body over there, which is after all apprehended as living body, must have this sense from an *apperceptive transfer* from my living body, and then in a way that excludes an actually direct, and thus primordial, demonstration of the predicates of specific bodiliness, a demonstration through proper perception. It is clear from the start that only a similarity connecting that body over there with my body within my primordial sphere can provide the motivational foundation for the *analogizing* apprehension of the former as another living body.
>
> It would therefore be a certain assimilating apperception, but not on that account by any means an inference by analogy. Apperception is no inference, no act of thought. Every apperception in which we apprehend pregiven objects at a glance, for instance the pregiven everyday world, and grasp them in noticing, understanding without further ado their sense with its horizons, points back intentionally to a *primal instituting* in which an object of similar sense had first constituted itself. Even the things of this world unknown to us are, generally speaking, known according to their type. We have seen the like before, though not precisely this thing here. So every everyday experience harbours an analogizing transfer of an originally instituted objective sense onto the new case, in its anticipating apprehension of the object as of similar sense. As far as pregivenness reaches, so far reaches such transfer; and what turns out in further experience to be actually new in the sense may then in turn function as instituting and found a pregivenness of richer sense. The child who already sees things understands, say, for the first time the purposive sense of scissors, and from then on sees scissors as such at the first glance without further ado; but of course not in explicit reproduction, comparison, and the carrying out of an inference. Yet the manner in which apperceptions arise, and in consequence point back intentionally, in themselves, through their sense and sense-horizon, to their genesis, varies greatly. To the levels of formation of objective senses correspond those of apperceptions. Ultimately we always come back to the radical distinction of apperceptions into those that by their genesis belong purely to the primordinal sphere, and those that appear with the sense *alter ego* and, thanks to a higher-level genesis, have built new sense upon this sense.

German:

> Erfahrung ist Originalbewußtsein, und in der Tat sagen wir im Falle der Erfahrung von einem Menschen allgemein, der Andere stehe selbst *leibhaftig* vor uns da. Anderseits hindert diese Leibhaftigkeit nicht, daß wir ohne weiteres zugestehen, daß dabei eigentlich nicht das andere Ich selbst, nicht seine Erlebnisse, seine Erscheinungen selbst, nichts von dem, was seinem Eigenwesen selbst angehört, zu ursprünglicher Gegebenheit komme. Wäre das der Fall, wäre das Eigenwesentliche des Anderen in direkter Weise zugänglich, so wäre es bloß Moment meines Eigenwesens und schließlich er selbst und ich selbst einerlei. Es verhielte sich ähnlich mit seinem Leib, wenn er nichts anderes wäre als der *Körper*, der rein in meinen wirklichen und möglichen Erfahrungen sich konstituierende Einheit ist, meiner primordialen Sphäre zugehörig als Gebilde ausschließlich meiner *Sinnlichkeit*. Eine gewisse *Mittelbarkeit der Intentionalität* muß hier vorliegen, und zwar von der jedenfalls beständig zugrunde liegenden Unterschicht der *primordialen Welt* auslaufend, die ein *Mit da* vorstellig macht, das doch nicht selbst da ist, nie ein Selbst-da werden kann. Es handelt sich also um eine Art des *Mitgegenwärtig-machens*, eine Art *Appräsentation*.
>
> Eine solche liegt schon in der äußeren Erfahrung vor, sofern die eigentlich gesehene Vorderseite eines Dinges stets und notwendig eine dingliche Rückseite appräsentiert, und ihr einen mehr oder minder bestimmten Gehalt vorzeichnet. Andrerseits kann es gerade diese Art der schon die primordiale Natur mitkonstituierenden Appräsentation nicht sein, da zu ihr die Möglichkeit der Bewährung durch entsprechende erfüllende Präsentation gehört (die Rückseite wird zur Vorderseite), während das für diejenige Appräsentation, die in eine andere Originalsphäre hineinleiten soll, a priori ausgeschlossen sein muß. Wie kann in der meinen die Appräsentation einer anderen und damit der Sinn *Anderer* motiviert sein, und in der Tat als Erfahrung, wie es schon das Wort Appräsentation (Als-mitgegenwärtig-bewußt-machen) andeutet? Eine beliebige Vergegenwärtigung kann das nicht. Sie kann das nur in Verflechtung mit einer Gegenwärtigung, einer eigentlichen Selbstgebung; und nur als durch sie gefordert kann sie den Charakter der Appräsentation haben, ähnlich wie in der Dingerfahrung wahrnehmungsmäßiges Dasein Mitdasein motiviert.
>
> Den Untergrund eigentlicher Wahrnehmung bietet uns die dem allgemeinen Rahmen der beständigen Selbstwahrnehmung des ego eingeordnete kontinuierlich fortgehende Wahrnehmung der primordial reduzierten Welt in der von uns früher beschriebenen Gliederung. Die Frage ist nun, was in dieser Hinsicht in Betracht kommen muß und wie die Motivation läuft, wie die recht komplizierte intentionale Leistung der faktisch zustandekommenden Appräsentation sich enthüllt.
>
> Eine erste Leitung kann uns der Wortsinn Anderer bieten — anderes Ich; alter sagt alter-ego, und das ego, das hier impliziert ist, das bin ich selbst, innerhalb meiner primordialen Eigenheit konstituiert, und zwar in Einzigkeit als psychophysische Einheit (als primordinaler Mensch), als *personales* Ich unmittelbar waltend in meinem, dem einzigen Leib, unmittelbar auch hineinwirkend in die primordiale Umwelt; im übrigen Subjekt eines konkreten intentionalen Lebens, einer auf sich selbst und auf die *Welt* bezogenen psychischen Sphäre. Das alles, und zwar in der im erfahrenen Leben erwachsenden Typisierung, mit den vertrauten Verlaufs- und Komplexionsformen, steht uns zu Gebote. Durch welche ihrerseits höchst komplizierte Intentionalitäten es sich konstituiert hat, das haben wir freilich nicht untersucht — es bildet eine eigene Schicht großer Untersuchungen, auf die wir nicht eingegangen sind und nicht eingehen konnten.
>
> Nehmen wir nun an, es tritt ein anderer Mensch in unseren Wahrnehmungsbereich, so heißt das, primordinal reduziert: es tritt im Wahrnehmungsbereich meiner primordialen Natur ein Körper auf, der als primordinaler natürlich bloß Bestimmungsstück meiner selbst (*immanente Transzendenz*) ist. Da in dieser Natur und Welt mein Leib der einzige Körper ist, der als Leib (fungierendes Organ) ursprünglich konstituiert ist und konstituiert sein kann, so muß der Körper dort, der als Leib doch aufgefaßt ist, diesen Sinn von einer *apperzeptiven Übertragung* von meinem Leib her haben, und dann in einer Weise, die eine wirklich direkte und somit primordiale Ausweisung der Prädikate der spezifischen Leiblichkeit, eine Ausweisung durch eigentliche Wahrnehmung, ausschließt. Es ist von vornherein klar, daß nur eine innerhalb meiner Primordialsphäre jenen Körper dort mit meinem Körper verbindende Ähnlichkeit das Motivationsfundament für die *analogisierende* Auffassung des ersteren als anderer Leib abgeben kann.
>
> Es wäre also eine gewisse verähnlichende Apperzeption, aber darum keineswegs ein Analogieschluß. Apperzeption ist kein Schluß, kein Denkakt. Jede Apperzeption, in der wir vorgegebene Gegenstände, etwa die vorgegebene Alltagswelt mit einem Blick auffassen und gewahrend erfassen, ohne weiteres ihren Sinn mit seinen Horizonten verstehen, weist intentional auf eine *Urstiftung* zurück, in der sich ein Gegenstand ähnlichen Sinnes erstmalig konstituiert hatte. Auch die uns unbekannten Dinge dieser Welt sind, allgemein zu reden, ihrem Typus nach bekannte. Wir haben dergleichen, obschon gerade nicht dieses Ding hier, früher schon gesehen. So birgt jede Alltagserfahrung eine analogisierende Übertragung eines ursprünglich gestifteten gegenständlichen Sinnes auf den neuen Fall, in seiner antizipierenden Auffassung des Gegenstandes als den ähnlichen Sinnes. Soweit Vorgegebenheit, soweit solche Übertragung, wobei dann wieder das sich in weiterer Erfahrung als wirklich neu Herausstellende des Sinnes wieder stiftend fungieren und eine Vorgegebenheit reicherer Sinnes fundieren mag. Das Kind, das schon Dinge sieht, versteht etwa erstmalig den Zwecksinn einer Schere, und von nun ab sieht es ohne weiteres im ersten Blick Scheren als solche; aber natürlich nicht in expliziter Reproduktion, Vergleichung und im Vollziehen eines Schlusses. Doch ist die Art, wie Apperzeptionen entspringen und in weiterer Folge in sich, durch ihren Sinn und Sinneshorizont, auf ihre Genesis intentional zurückweisen, eine sehr verschiedene. Den Stufenbildungen der gegenständlichen Sinne entsprechen die der Apperzeptionen. Letztlich kommen wir immer zurück auf die radikale Unterscheidung der Apperzeptionen in solche, die ihrer Genesis nach rein der primordinalen Sphäre zugehören, und solche, die mit dem Sinn *alter ego* auftreten und auf diesem Sinn dank einer höherstufigen Genesis neuen Sinn aufgestuft haben.

*Note on the text.* The quotation gives §50 from its second paragraph to its end; the first paragraph (Hua I 138) only says that the real difficulties lie in the step to the other. The two page breaks (Hua I 139/140 inside "Den Untergrund eigentlicher Wahrnehmung bietet uns die dem / allgemeinen Rahmen", and 140/141 between paragraphs) lose nothing. "eine Vorgegebenheit reicherer Sinnes" is as in the file, where the grammar asks for *reicheren*; the sense, "of richer sense", is not in doubt. The apparatus has no notes on these pages.

The first sentence states the norm and the second concedes what breaks it. "Experience is original consciousness" (*Originalbewußtsein*), and we say that the other stands there "*in person*" (*leibhaftig*, italic). The word is the ordinary German for "in the flesh", and *Leib* is inside it. Husserl keeps it and pulls it apart from the "original givenness" it would usually name. The other is there in person, yet "nothing of what belongs to his own essence" (*seinem Eigenwesen*) comes to original givenness. What is in person is a living body. The italics fall on the word whose two senses the section separates.

Then comes the argument that makes the mediacy necessary rather than a defect, and it is a counterfactual. "Were that the case", were the other's own accessible directly, "it would be merely a moment of my own essence, and in the end he himself and I myself would be one and the same" (*er selbst und ich selbst einerlei*). A directly given other would not be other. The second counterfactual applies the same reasoning to the body: if his living body were only the "*body*" (*Körper*) constituted in my "*sensibility*", it would be mine in the same way. So "a certain *mediacy of intentionality*" "must" be present. The "must" follows from the sense *other*, not from a limit of my powers. The new terms are built to say presence without self-presence. The "*co-there*" (*Mit da*) is "not itself there" and "can never become an itself-there" (*nie ein Selbst-da werden kann*). The hyphenated "*making co-present*" (*Mitgegenwärtig-machen*) explains the Latin-derived *Appräsentation*, and Husserl glosses his own word a second time in parentheses: "making-conscious-as-co-present" (*Als-mitgegenwärtig-bewußt-machen*). The second gloss adds what the first lacks, the "as" (*Als*). The other is not made present. He is made conscious *as* co-present, and the appresentation includes in its own sense that it can never become a presentation.

The comparison with the back side of a thing fixes that sense by contrast. Thing perception appresents too, but its appresentation can be verified "through a corresponding fulfilling presentation (the back side becomes the front side)". For the appresentation of another sphere "this must be excluded a priori". The difficulty is in the phrase "and indeed as experience" (*und in der Tat als Erfahrung*). An appresentation that never fulfils itself looks like phantasy or empty supposition. Husserl's answer is that it is "demanded" (*gefordert*) by a presentation with which it is interwoven, as the thing's front side demands a back side. The section does not yet say how an appresentation can be verified when it can never be fulfilled. That is §52's task, "appresentation as a kind of experience with its own style of verification", and §52 finds the model in memory: as my past constitutes itself in my living present through concordant recollections, the other constitutes itself in my primordial sphere through appresentations (Hua I 145).

The decisive paragraph turns on two modal words. Because my living body is "the only" body originally constituted as living body (row 25), the body over there "must" (*muß*) have its sense from "an *apperceptive transfer*". That step follows from §44's definition. The next does not follow in the same way: "It is clear from the start (*Es ist von vornherein klar*) that only a similarity … can provide the motivational foundation." The phrase claims evidence where the argument supplies only an elimination. Within the primordial sphere nothing else connects the two bodies, so similarity is the only candidate. Whether a similarity of bodies seen from outside can motivate the transfer of a sense that my own body has from within is the question §§51–54 have to answer, and §54 answers it by changing the terms of the similarity: the body over there resembles not my body Here but how it would look "*if I were there*" (Hua I 147; row 27).

The last paragraph opens in the subjunctive, "It would therefore be" (*Es wäre also*), and names the result twice: "a certain assimilating apperception, but not on that account by any means an inference by analogy" (*verähnlichende Apperzeption, aber darum keineswegs ein Analogieschluß*). "Not on that account" (*darum*) blocks the step from "analogizing" to "by analogy". An inference by analogy would have premises (this body is like mine, mine expresses an inner life) and a conclusion (that body expresses one too). It would be a judgement, and its conclusion could at best be probable. "Apperception is no inference, no act of thought." The analogy is at work in the sense of what is seen, not in a reasoning about it. The proof is that every everyday experience works this way, and the term that carries the proof is "primal instituting" (*Urstiftung*, italic). An object of similar sense "had first constituted itself" once, and the sense "originally instituted" (*ursprünglich gestifteten*) is carried over to each new case. The child who has once understood scissors "sees scissors as such at the first glance", "not in explicit reproduction, comparison, and the carrying out of an inference". The example works by making the inference look absurd. Nobody infers that the thing in front of him is a pair of scissors.

The example is also where the analogy with the experience of others is weakest, and the paragraph's last sentences start to say so. Apperceptions "vary greatly" in how they point back to their genesis, and there is "a radical distinction" between those that belong purely to the primordial sphere and those "with the sense *alter ego*", which rest on "a higher-level genesis". §51 names the two differences at once (Hua I 141–142). In the experience of others "the primally instituting original is constantly alive and present" (*das urstiftende Original immerfort lebendig gegenwärtig*): my own living body is not a past first case, like the first pair of scissors, but always there alongside the body over there. And what is appresented "can never actually come to presence". Scissors seen at a glance can be picked up and used, and the transferred sense is fulfilled. The other's experiences never are. The scissors show that apperception is not inference. They do not show what kind of apperception the experience of others is, and the section's own last sentence leaves that to the "higher-level genesis".

The vocabulary of institution has a history in the Meditations, and §50 is where it becomes explicit. §27 said that every evidence "institutes" (*stiftet*) a lasting possession (Hua I 95; row 17); §33 that explication "institutes a habituality" (Hua I 102; row 19); §§38–39 traced the passive genesis behind every pregiven object (rows 21–22). §50 names the act at the root of these, the *Urstiftung*, and uses it to place the experience of others in the general economy of sense. The word returns once more, at the end of the Meditation.
