In §62 the beginning phenomenologist is one "who is only just bringing the phenomenological reduction to primal instituting (*zur Urstiftung*) as a universal habitus of constitutive research" (Hua I 176; row 30). Phenomenology itself has the structure §50 describes, a sense first instituted and then carried forward.

The first version of the Fifth Meditation, written in 1929, has the scissors and the denial of inference, with a hedge the printed text drops: "In a certain way every apperception of something new on the basis of an earlier primally instituting apperception is analogizing transfer, but no inference by analogy" (*In gewisser Weise ist jede Apperzeption eines Neuen … analogisierende Übertragung, aber kein Analogieschluss*; Text Nr. 1, §7, Hua XV 14, read in `husserl-hua-15-…`). The same draft explains the appresentation as an "empty presentification" that can be made intuitive only somewhat "as I can have my past intuitively only in the form of a recollective presentification" (Hua XV 14). The Meditations drop "in a certain way" and make the transfer universal ("So every everyday experience harbours…"). They lead with the back side of a thing, which is verifiable, and move the memory analogy, which is not verifiable in the same way, to §52. The revision makes the experience of others one case of a general law of apperception, and it puts the difference, the original that is always alive and the presentation that never comes, into §51. That is where §50 leaves the understanding. The experience of others is perception-like because it is apperception, and it is unlike any other apperception because its primal instituting never lies in the past and its fulfilment never arrives.

## §54, Hua I 147–148: the other as an ego in the mode There

Reads [§54, Paragraph 1](../commentaries/husserl/husserliana-1-cartesianische-meditationen-commentary.md#explication-of-the-sense-of-the-foreign-experiencing-appresentation).

The other is appresented as an ego "in the mode There". The appresentation does not copy my ego into the body over there, because the association that founds it is not direct. The body there does not pair with my body as it appears to me Here. It awakens the appearance my body would have "*if I were there*". Since my whole primordial ownness "has the content of the Here", an ego appresented There cannot be mine: Here and There exclude each other, and the same ego cannot be in both. The appresented ego is therefore another ego. The subjunctive carries the argument. The other is given through what I would be if I were where I am not, and that is why the experience of others is no projection of myself.

> What has just been shown evidently points to the course of the association constituting the mode *other*. It is not an immediate one. The body belonging to my primordinal surrounding world (the body of the later other) is for me a body in the mode *There*. Its manner of appearance does not pair in direct association with the manner of appearance that my living body actually has at any time (in the mode *Here*). Rather, it reproductively awakens a similar appearance, one belonging to the constitutive system of my living body as a body in space. It recalls my bodily look *if I were there*. Here too pairing takes place, although the awakening does not become an intuition of memory. Into it enters not only the manner of appearance of my body that is first awakened, but my body itself as the synthetic unity of this and of its manifold other familiar manners of appearance. So the assimilating apperception becomes possible and founded through which the outer body there receives analogically from my own the sense *living body*, and in further consequence the sense living body of another *world*, by analogy with my primordinal one. The general style of this apperception, as of every apperception that grows associatively, is accordingly to be described as follows. With the associative coincidence of the data founding the apperception, a higher-level association takes place. If the one datum is one of the manners of appearance of an intentional object, an index for an associatively awakened system of manifold appearances in which it would show itself, then the other datum too is completed into the appearance of something, namely of an analogous object. But it is not as if the unity and multiplicity *transferred over* onto it merely completed it with manners of appearance from the first. Rather, the analogically apprehended object, or its indicated system of appearances, is analogically adapted to the analogous appearance that co-awakened this whole system. Every transfer at a distance that grows from associative pairing is at once fusion and, so far as incompatibilities do not intervene, assimilation, the adaptation of the sense of the one to that of the other.
>
> If we now go back to our case, the apperception of the *alter ego*, it is now self-evident that what is appresented on the part of that *body* there, in my primordial *surrounding world*, is not my psychic being, nothing at all from my sphere of ownness. I am bodily here, centre of a primordial *world* oriented around me. So my entire primordial ownness as monad has the content of the Here, and not that of any There whatever, and so not that of this determinate There, which varies in any *I can and I do* that may be inserted. The one and the other exclude each other; they cannot be at the same time. But since the foreign body in the There enters into a pairing association with my body in the Here and, because it is perceptually given, becomes the kernel of an appresentation, the experience of a co-existing ego, this ego must, according to the whole sense-giving course of the association, necessarily be appresented as an ego co-existing now in the mode There (*as if I were there*). My own ego, the ego given in constant self-perception, is however now actual with the content of its Here. So an ego is appresented as other. What is primordially incompatible in coexistence becomes compatible in that my primordial ego constitutes [the ego that is] other for it through an appresentative apperception which, according to its peculiar character, never demands or admits fulfilment through presentation.

German:

> Das soeben Aufgewiesene deutet offenbar auf den Gang der den Modus *Anderer* konstituierenden Assoziation. Sie ist keine unmittelbare. Der meiner primordinalen Umwelt angehörige Körper (des nachmals Anderen) ist für mich Körper im Modus *Dort*. Seine Erscheinungsweise paart sich nicht in direkter Assoziation mit der Erscheinungsweise, die mein Leib jeweils wirklich hat (im Modus *Hier*), sondern sie weckt reproduktiv eine ähnliche, zum konstitutiven System meines Leibes als Körper im Raum gehörige Erscheinung. Sie erinnert an mein körperliches Aussehen, *wenn ich dort wäre*. Auch hier, obschon die Weckung nicht zu einer Erinnerungs-Anschauung wird, vollzieht sich Paarung. In sie tritt nicht nur die zunächst geweckte Erscheinungsweise meines Körpers sondern er selbst als synthetische Einheit dieser und seiner mannigfaltigen anderen vertrauten Erscheinungsweisen. So wird die verähnlichende Apperzeption möglich und begründet, durch welche der äußere Körper dort von dem mir eigenen analogisch den Sinn Leib erhält; in weiterer Folge den Sinn Leib einer anderen *Welt* nach Analogie meiner primordinalen. Der allgemeine Stil dieser wie jeder assoziativ erwachsenden Apperzeption ist danach so zu beschreiben: Mit der assoziativen Deckung der die Apperzeption fundierenden Daten vollzieht sich eine höherstufige Assoziation. Ist das eine Datum eine der Erscheinungsweisen eines intentionalen Gegenstandes — eines Index für ein assoziativ gewecktes System von mannigfaltigen Erscheinungen, als in welchen er sich selbst zeigen würde —, so wird das andere Datum ebenfalls zur Erscheinung von etwas, und zwar eines analogen Gegenstandes ergänzt. Es ist aber nicht so, als ob die ihm *überschobene* Einheit und Mannigfaltigkeit bloß ihn ergänzte durch Erscheinungsweisen von dieser anderen her; vielmehr ist der analogisch aufgefaßte Gegenstand bzw. sein indiziertes Erscheinungssystem eben analogisch angepaßt der analogen Erscheinung, die dieses ganze System mit weckte. Jede durch assoziative Paarung erwachsende Fernüberschiebung ist zugleich Verschmelzung und in ihr, soweit nicht Unverträglichkeiten eingreifen, Verähnlichung, Angleichung des Sinnes der einen an den des anderen.
>
> Gehen wir nun zu unserem Fall der Apperzeption des alter ego zurück, so ist es jetzt selbstverständlich, daß, was da appräsentiert ist von seiten jenes *Körpers* dort, in meiner primordialen *Umwelt* nicht mein Psychisches, nichts überhaupt aus meiner Eigenheitssphäre ist. Ich bin leiblich hier, Zentrum einer um mich orientierten primordialen *Welt*. Damit hat meine gesamte primordiale Eigenheit als Monade den Gehalt des Hier, und nicht den irgendeines und so auch jenes bestimmten Dort, der sich in irgendeinem einzusetzenden *Ich kann und ich tue* abwandelt. Eines und das andere schließt sich aus, es kann nicht zugleich sein. Indem aber der fremde Körper im Dort eine paarende Assoziation eingeht mit meinem Körper im Hier, und weil er wahrnehmungsmäßig gegeben ist, zum Kern einer Appräsentation wird, der Erfahrung eines mitdaseienden ego, muß dieses nach dem ganzen sinngebenden Gang der Assoziation notwendig appräsentiert sein als jetzt mitdaseiendes ego im Modus Dort (*wie wenn ich dort wäre*). Mein eigenes ego, das in ständiger Selbstwahrnehmung gegebene, ist aber jetzt aktuell mit dem Gehalt seines Hier. Es ist also ein ego als anderes appräsentiert. Das primordial Unverträgliche in der Koexistenz wird verträglich dadurch, daß mein primordiales ego für das andere ego durch eine appräsentative Apperzeption konstituiert, die ihrer Eigenart gemäß nie Erfüllung durch Präsentation fordert und zuläßt.

*Note on the text.* The page break (Hua I 147/148) falls between the two paragraphs and loses nothing. In the last sentence the file has "daß mein primordiales ego für das andere ego … konstituiert", in which *konstituiert* [constitutes] has no object; the sense needs something like "the ego that is other for it". The English supplies that in brackets, and the reading does not rest on the clause's construction. The apparatus records that the manuscript ordered the clause on the There differently (148,7–9): "und nicht den in irgendeinem einzusetzenden „Ich kann und ich tue“ sich abwandelnden irgendeines und so auch jenes bestimmten Dort" [and not that of any There whatever, and so of this determinate There, varying in any "I can and I do" to be inserted]. The manuscript makes clear what the printed relative clause leaves open: it is the There that varies with what I can do.

The first sentences deny the obvious model and put a subjunctive in its place. "It is not an immediate one" (*Sie ist keine unmittelbare*). The body over there does not pair "in direct association" with the appearance my body "actually has at any time (in the mode *Here*)". If it did, the pairing would join two unlike things. My body is given to me Here as a zero point, felt from within and seen only in part, and the other's body is given There as a whole thing in space. Instead the appearance there "reproductively awakens" a similar appearance that belongs "to the constitutive system of my living body as a body in space". The verb that follows is a memory word: "It recalls" (*Sie erinnert an*) "my bodily look *if I were there*" (*mein körperliches Aussehen,* wenn ich dort wäre, italic). Husserl qualifies it at once: "the awakening does not become an intuition of memory" (*Erinnerungs-Anschauung*). Nothing is recalled, because I have never seen my body from there. What is awakened is a possibility of my body, the one §53 established: "I can … transform every *There* into a *Here*", and from there "I would see the same things" in other manners of appearance (Hua I 146). The subjunctive "were" (*wäre*) stands for a capacity, not for a past.

Here the inference is weaker than its wording. §53 secured that I could see *things* from there. §54 needs that my *body* has a look from there, and the "constitutive system of my body as a body in space" is supposed to supply it. But if I go there, I take my body with me, and I see it from there as I see it here, from within and in part. The look my body would have from there, as an outer body, is the look of a body in whose place I would be. That is the look the other's body already has. The step is carried by the phrase "my living body as a body in space" (*meines Leibes als Körper im Raum*), which §53 had grounded only in the capacity to move: my body "is apprehended and apprehensible as a natural body existing in space like any other" because I can take up every place (Hua I 146). The appearance the pairing needs is a construction of my capacities, not an appearance I have had.

The general description that follows names the mechanism with a run of nouns: "transfer at a distance" (*Fernüberschiebung*), "fusion" (*Verschmelzung*), "assimilation" (*Verähnlichung*), "adaptation of the sense" (*Angleichung des Sinnes*). The weight is on the correction inside it: "But it is not as if" the transferred unity "merely completed" the second datum from the first. Rather the analogically apprehended object "is analogically adapted to the analogous appearance". The transfer goes both ways. What I carry over to the body there is adjusted to what appears there. And the qualification "so far as incompatibilities do not intervene" (*soweit nicht Unverträglichkeiten eingreifen*) prepares the second paragraph, where the incompatibility is the point.

The second paragraph begins with a "self-evident" (*selbstverständlich*) that, unlike §42's, Husserl means. What is appresented "is not my psychic being, nothing at all from my sphere of ownness". The reason is the Here: "I am bodily here, centre of a primordial *world* oriented around me." From that follows the sentence the row names: "my entire primordial ownness as monad has the content of the Here" (*den Gehalt des Hier*). It is a strong formula. Ownness is not only located Here. Its whole content is Here, including every There I could make into a Here by moving. Every There I can reach by "I can and I do" (*Ich kann und ich tue*, italic) is a There of mine, a place where I could be. The other's There is not one of them, because the ego appresented there is "now" (*jetzt*) There while I am "now actual with the content of its Here". "The one and the other exclude each other; they cannot be at the same time." The argument is about simultaneity. I could be There later, but not There and Here now, so an ego co-existing There now is not me.

The paragraph's conclusion is short: "So an ego is appresented as other" (*Es ist also ein ego als anderes appräsentiert*). Husserl gives the subjunctive a second form here. In the first paragraph it was "*if I were there*" (*wenn ich dort wäre*), the look of my body. Now it is "*as if I were there*" (*wie wenn ich dort wäre*), the mode of the other ego. The "as if" is not the "as if" of a fiction, which §22 refused for the idea of the world (row 15). It says that the other ego is given as having its Here where I have a There, as the centre of a world oriented around it. The last sentence turns the incompatibility into the structure of the experience: "What is primordially incompatible in coexistence becomes compatible" because the appresentation "never demands or admits fulfilment through presentation". An ego that could be presented would have to be mine, Here. An ego that is only appresented can be There and now. §50 had made the impossibility of fulfilment part of appresentation's sense (row 26). §54 shows why the impossibility is needed. It is what lets two Heres coexist.

The context shows where this leads. The next paragraphs build the other's psychic life from the body outward. First come the "members as hands functioning in touching or pushing, as feet functioning in walking, as eyes functioning in seeing" (Hua I 148), the organs of §44 (row 25) now understood in another, and the ego "at first determined only as governing bodily in this way". Then come higher contents, the look "of the angry man, of the cheerful man", understood "from my own comportment under similar circumstances" (Hua I 149). And because "every pairing association is reciprocal", understanding the other "discloses my own psychic life according to similarity and otherness" (Hua I 149). §55 then identifies the body that I see There with the body the other has Here as one body in two manners of givenness (Hua I 156), and from that identification it builds the first form of objectivity, a nature common to both. The Here/There pair returns in §62, where the other constitutes itself in me "*appresentatively* mirrored, and not as original" (Hua I 175; row 30). The "not as original" is the "never demands or admits fulfilment" of §54.

A text of August 1931 on empathy, which Husserl filed with the Cartesian Meditations material, spells out the subjunctive more plainly (*Zur Phänomenologie der Intersubjektivität III*, Text Nr. 15, Hua XV 257, read in `husserl-hua-15-…`). There "a governing can be present only as my governing in original perception", and so a governing in the outer body can only be appresented. "The body there resembles my body as if I were there." It "presentifies … the self-appearance that I would have if I went there and were in the place of this thing" (*die ich haben würde, wenn ich dorthin ginge und an Stelle dieses Dinges wäre*), and so presents "the being-outside-me of a body in whose place I could be there". The later text names what §54 leaves implicit, the step from going there to being in the place of the other body. The appearance of my body from There is the appearance of a body in whose place I could stand, and that is exactly the appearance of the other's body. That is where the understanding ends. The other is given through my capacity to be elsewhere, and he is other because he is there now, where I am not.

## §60, Hua I 166–168: compossibility without creation

Reads [§60, Paragraph 1](../commentaries/husserl/husserliana-1-cartesianische-meditationen-commentary.md#metaphysical-results-of-our-interpretation-of-the-experience-of-others).

§60 takes Leibniz's doctrine of compossibility and moves its ground. For Leibniz, infinitely many worlds are possible and God could have created any one of them, but not several at once, because they are incompossible. Husserl agrees, "Leibniz is right", and then puts the ego where Leibniz had God. The possibilities are the variations I can make of myself in free variation. Each of them cancels every other, and each is cancelled by the ego I actually am. Two worlds are incompossible "in the same way" as two variants of my ego. The uniqueness of the world is not the result of a choice among worlds that could have been created. It follows from the fact that every conceivable plurality of monads is conceived by me, and so stands in community with me. The ego does not create, though: "I can only find" the others. That is the sense in which Husserl calls these results "metaphysical": they are ultimate cognitions of being, reached by explicating the ego and not by constructing a world beyond it.

> They are metaphysical, if it is true that ultimate cognitions of being are to be called metaphysical. But nothing less than metaphysics in the customary sense is in question here, which is a historically degenerate metaphysics, one that is anything but in accordance with the sense in which metaphysics was originally instituted as *first philosophy*. Phenomenology's purely intuitive, concrete and moreover apodictic manner of verification excludes all *metaphysical adventures*, all speculative extravagances. Let us bring out some of our metaphysical results, adding further consequences. My ego, given to myself apodictically, the only one that I can posit as existing in absolute apodicticity, can a priori be a world-experiencing ego only by being in community with others of its kind, a member of a community of monads given as oriented from it. Consistent self-verification of the objective world of experience implies consistent self-verification of other monads as existing. Conversely, no plurality of monads is thinkable for me except as an explicitly or implicitly communalized one. That includes: one that constitutes an objective world in itself and spatializes, temporalizes and realizes itself in that world, as animal and in particular human beings. The being-together of monads, their mere being-at-once, means with essential necessity being temporally at once, and then also being temporalized in the form of *real* temporality.
>
> But further metaphysical results of the highest importance follow from this. Is it conceivable (for me, who say this, and from me again for anyone conceivable who may say it) that several separate pluralities of monads, that is, pluralities not communalized with one another, coexist, each of which therefore constitutes a world of its own, so that there are two worlds separated into infinity, two infinite spaces and space-times? Obviously this is, instead of a conceivability, a pure countersense. A priori, each such group of monads, as the unity of an intersubjectivity, and of one that may lack any actual relation of community with the other, has of course its own world, which may look quite different. But these two worlds are then necessarily mere surrounding worlds of these intersubjectivities and mere aspects of a single objective world common to them. For the two intersubjectivities do not stand in the air. As thought up by me, they both stand in necessary community with me as the constituting primal monad for them (or with me in a possible variation of myself). So they belong in truth to a single all-community that encloses me myself and comprehends in one all the monads and groups of monads thinking [*sic*] as coexisting. So there can in reality be only a single community of monads, that of all coexisting monads, and accordingly only a single objective world, only a single objective time, only one objective space, only one nature; and if structures are laid down in me at all that imply the being-with of the other monads, there must be this one single nature. Only this is possible: that different groups of monads and worlds stand to one another as those that may belong to the star-worlds invisible to us stand to us, that is, with animals that lack any actual connection with us. But their worlds are surrounding worlds with open horizons that are only factually, only contingently, not disclosable for them.
>
> But the sense of this uniqueness of the monadological world, and of the objective world *innate* to it, must be rightly understood. Of course Leibniz is right when he says that infinitely many monads and groups of monads are thinkable, but that not all these possibilities are therefore compossible; and again, that infinitely many worlds could have been *created*, but not several at once, since they are incompossible. It is to be noted here that I can first think myself, this apodictic-factual ego, otherwise in free variation, and so gain the system of the possible variations of myself, each of which, however, is cancelled by every other and by the ego that I actually am. It is a system of a priori incompossibility. Further, the fact *I am* prescribes whether, and which, other monads are others for me. I can only find them; I cannot create which ones are to be for me. If I think myself over into a pure possibility, it in turn prescribes which monads are others for it. And so, going further, I recognize that every monad that holds as a concrete possibility prescribes a compossible universe, a closed *world of monads*, and that two worlds of monads are incompossible in the same way as two possible variations of my ego, and likewise of any ego at all that is thought of as presupposed.

German:

> Sie sind metaphysisch, wenn es wahr ist, daß letzte Seinserkenntnisse metaphysische zu nennen sind. Aber nichts weniger als Metaphysik im gewohnten Sinne ist hier in Frage, als welche eine historisch entartete Metaphysik ist, die nichts weniger als dem Sinn gemäß ist, mit dem Metaphysik als *Erste Philosophie* ursprünglich gestiftet worden war. Die rein intuitive, konkrete und zudem apodiktische Ausweisungsart der Phänomenologie schließt alle *metaphysischen Abenteuer*, alle spekulativen Überschwenglichkeiten aus. Heben wir einiges aus unseren metaphysischen Ergebnissen heraus unter Beifügung weiterer Konsequenzen. Mein mir selbst apodiktisch gegebenes *ego*, das einzige in absoluter Apodiktizität von mir als seiend zu setzende, kann a priori nur welterfahrendes *ego* sein, indem es mit anderen seinesgleichen in Gemeinschaft ist, Glied einer von ihm aus orientiert gegebenen Monadengemeinschaft. Konsequentes Sich-ausweisen der objektiven Erfahrungswelt impliziert konsequentes Sich-ausweisen von anderen Monaden als seienden. Umgekehrt ist keine Monadenmehrheit für mich denkbar denn als eine explizite oder implicite vergemeinschaftete; darin liegt: eine objektive Welt in sich konstituierende und in ihr sich selbst — als animalische und im besonderen menschliche Wesen — verräumlichende, verzeitlichende, realisierende. Das Zusammensein von Monaden, ihr bloßes Zugleichsein bedeutet wesensnotwendig Zeitlich-zugleichsein, und dann auch Verzeitlicht-sein in der Form *realer* Zeitlichkeit.
>
> Aber daran schließen sich noch weitere höchst wichtige metaphysische Ergebnisse. Ist es erdenklich (für mich, der ich das sage, und von mir aus wieder für jeden Erdenklichen, der das sagen mag), daß mehrere getrennte, d.i. miteinander nicht vergemeinschaftete Monadenvielheiten koexistieren, deren jede also eine eigene Welt konstituiert, also zwei ins Unendliche getrennte Welten, zwei unendliche Räume und Raum-Zeiten? Offenbar ist das statt einer Erdenklichkeit ein purer Widersinn. A priori hat jede solche Monadengruppe freilich als Einheit einer Intersubjektivität, und einer möglicherweise jeder aktuellen Gemeinschaftsbeziehung mit der anderen entbehrenden, ihre möglicherweise ganz anders aussehende Welt. Aber diese beiden Welten sind dann notwendig bloße Umwelten dieser Intersubjektivitäten und bloße Aspekte einer einzigen, ihnen gemeinsamen objektiven Welt. Denn die beiden Intersubjektivitäten stehen nicht in der Luft; als von mir erdachte stehen sie beide mit mir als der konstituierenden Urmonade für sie (bzw. mit mir in einer Möglichkeitsabwandlung meiner selbst) in notwendiger Gemeinschaft. Sie gehören also in Wahrheit in eine einzige, mich selbst umschließende Allgemeinschaft, die alle als koexistierend denkenden Monaden und Monadengruppen in eins faßt. Es kann also nur eine einzige Monadengemeinschaft, die aller koexistierenden Monaden, in Wirklichkeit geben, demnach nur eine einzige objektive Welt, nur eine einzige objektive Zeit, nur einen objektiven Raum, nur eine Natur, und es muß, wenn überhaupt in mir Strukturen angelegt sind, die das Mit-sein der anderen Monaden implizieren, diese eine einzige Natur geben. Nur das ist möglich, daß verschiedene Monadengruppen und Welten so zueinander stehen, wie die den für uns unsichtbaren Gestirnwelten eventuell zugehörigen zu uns selbst stehen, also mit Animalien, die jedes aktuellen Konnexes mit uns entbehren. Ihre Welten sind aber Umwelten mit offenen, nur faktisch, nur zufälligerweise für sie nicht erschließbaren Horizonten.
>
> Der Sinn dieser Einzigkeit der monadologischen Welt und der ihr *eingeborenen* objektiven Welt muß aber recht verstanden werden. Natürlich hat Leibniz recht, wenn er sagt, daß unendlich viele Monaden und Monadengruppen denkbar sind, aber darum nicht alle diese Möglichkeiten kompossibel, und wieder, daß unendlich viele Welten hätten *geschaffen* sein können, aber nicht mehrere zugleich, da sie inkompossibel sind. Es ist hier zu beachten, daß ich zunächst mich selbst, dieses apodiktisch-faktische ego, in freier Variation umdenken kann, und so das System der Möglichkeitsabwandlungen meiner selbst gewinnen, deren jede aber durch jede andere und durch das ego, das ich wirklich bin, aufgehoben ist. Es ist ein System apriorischer Inkompossibilität. Ferner, das Faktum *Ich bin* schreibt vor, ob und welche anderen Monaden für mich andere sind; ich kann sie nur finden, aber nicht, welche für mich sein sollen, schaffen. Denke ich mich in eine reine Möglichkeit um, so schreibt auch sie wieder vor, welche Monaden für sie als andere sind. Und so weitergehend erkenne ich, daß jede Monade, die als konkrete Möglichkeit Geltung hat, ein kompossibles Universum, eine geschlossene *Monadenwelt* vorzeichnet, und daß zwei Monadenwelten in derselben Art inkompossibel sind wie zwei Möglichkeitsabwandlungen meines ego und ebenso irgendeines vorausgesetzt gedachten ego überhaupt.

*Note on the text.* The second and third paragraphs keep the print's short hyphenated lines in the file; the quotation joins them. The file reads "die alle als koexistierend denkenden Monaden" [all the monads thinking as coexisting]. The sense needs something like "zu denkenden" [to be thought], all the monads to be thought as coexisting. The English marks the file's word with *sic*, and the reading does not rest on it. The apparatus has no notes for Hua I 166–168, and the page breaks (166/167 between the first two paragraphs, 167/168 inside the third, after "das ego, das ich wirklich") lose nothing.

The first sentence is a conditional, and the conditional does the work. Husserl does not say that the results are metaphysics. He says they are metaphysical "if it is true that ultimate cognitions of being are to be called metaphysical". The name is claimed by definition, and the definition is then defended against the name's current use. "Nothing less than metaphysics in the customary sense" (*nichts weniger als Metaphysik im gewohnten Sinne*) is in question. The idiom returns at once in the relative clause: customary metaphysics is "anything but in accordance with" (*nichts weniger als … gemäß*) the sense in which metaphysics was "originally instituted" (*ursprünglich gestiftet*) as first philosophy. The doubled "nothing less than" splits the word in two, a degenerate historical sense and an instituted original one, and Husserl claims the second by going back past the first. The verb belongs to the word family of *Stiftung* [instituting], which §50 used for the primal instituting of every apperception (row 26). Metaphysics too has a primal instituting, and phenomenology claims to renew it. What it excludes is named in italics: "*metaphysical adventures*" (*metaphysischen Abenteuer*), "speculative extravagances". An adventure goes beyond what can be verified. The results that follow are to be read as explications of what verification already holds: "consistent self-verification" (*Konsequentes Sich-ausweisen*) of the world "implies" the self-verification of other monads.

The second paragraph argues for the uniqueness of the world through a question whose parenthesis decides the answer. "Is it conceivable (for me, who say this, and from me again for anyone conceivable who may say it)…?" Conceivability is always conceivability for someone, and the parenthesis names the someone before the question is put. The verdict follows: "instead of a conceivability, a pure countersense" (*Widersinn*), the word §41 kept for the problem of transcendence and §42 for "metaphysical" hypotheses (row 23; row 24). The reason is given in a figure: "the two intersubjectivities do not stand in the air" (*stehen nicht in der Luft*). In §34 the eidos *perception* floated "so to speak in the air of absolutely pure conceivabilities", freed from every fact (Hua I 105; row 20). Here the conceived intersubjectivities are refused that freedom. "As thought up by me" (*als von mir erdachte*), they stand in community with me "as the constituting primal monad for them". The parenthesis "(or with me in a possible variation of myself)" covers the eidetic case. Even when I vary myself into a pure possibility, the conceiver is still a variant of me, and what he conceives stands in community with him.

The inference is weaker than its "obviously" (*Offenbar*). It shows that whatever I conceive is conceived in relation to me. It shows that two separate worlds are not separate *for me*. It does not show that they cannot coexist without me, unless "to exist" means "to be verifiable for me". §41 had argued for that equation, and §60 uses it without restating it. The argument is the transcendental idealism of §41 applied to the plurality of worlds. Husserl then concedes what the argument cannot exclude. Groups of monads may lack "any actual connection" with us, like animals on "the star-worlds invisible to us" (*für uns unsichtbaren Gestirnwelten*). Their worlds are still "surrounding worlds" (*Umwelten*), with horizons "only factually, only contingently" (*nur faktisch, nur zufälligerweise*) closed to them. The doubled "only" carries the concession. The unity of the world is a unity of possible connection, and it survives the lack of any actual one.

The third paragraph turns from the conclusion to its sense, and it does so through Leibniz. The italic "*innate*" (*eingeborenen*) says that the objective world is not added to the world of monads from outside, as something they would then have to agree about. It is born in them. Leibniz is granted two claims. Infinitely many monads are thinkable "but not therefore all compossible", and infinitely many worlds "could have been *created*" but not several at once. The italic *geschaffen* [created] marks the word as Leibniz's, and the rest of the paragraph replaces it. In place of God's understanding, which surveys possible worlds, Husserl puts "free variation" of "this apodictic-factual ego". In place of the incompossibility of worlds, he puts the incompossibility of my variants: "each of which is cancelled by every other and by the ego that I actually am". I cannot be two of my possible selves at once, just as, in §54, I cannot be Here and There at once: "they cannot be at the same time" (Hua I 148; row 27). In place of creation he puts finding: "I can only find them; I cannot create which ones are to be for me." The ego that constitutes the sense of the others does not choose which others there are. "The fact *I am* prescribes" (*schreibt vor*) them. The two most theological terms in Leibniz's doctrine, the creator and the choice among worlds, are the two Husserl leaves out. What remains of the doctrine is the logic of the possible, now grounded in the variation of an ego that is given as a fact.

The context shows why the argument needs this ground. §59 had closed by promising that the return to constitution would show its worth in "the *monadological* results of our investigation" (Hua I 166). §60's heading renames them "metaphysical". The monads were already introduced "with Leibniz's word" in §33 (row 19), and §49 had denied that the "harmony of monads" was a "*metaphysical* substruction", "as little as the monads themselves are metaphysical inventions or hypotheses" (Hua I 138). §56 had called me "the primal monad" (*Urmonade*) from which I gain the others (Hua I 159). §60 draws the consequence for the world as a whole. The paragraph after the passage says what it opens: questions become meaningful "(whatever their answer may be)" that tradition had to place "beyond all scientific limits" (Hua I 168). §61 names the first of them, the generative problems of birth, death and the connection of generations (Hua I 169). §64 names the rest, "contingent facticity, death, fate", the sense of history, the "ethical-religious problems", and insists that phenomenology excludes only naive metaphysics, "*not* … metaphysics as such" (Hua I 182). The conditional of §60's first sentence becomes §64's claim. The same section had already used the logic of compossibility in a smaller frame. In §36 not all possible types of experience are "*compossible*" in one ego, "not in any order, at any place of its own temporality" (Hua I 108), and §37 makes the universe of experiences compossible "only in the universal unity-form of streaming" (Hua I 110; row 21). §60 extends that rule from experiences in one ego to monads in one world.

The corpus holds an earlier version of the argument. A manuscript of 1 September 1921, "Can there be separate subjects related to separate worlds?" (*Zur Phänomenologie der Intersubjektivität II*, Text Nr. 4, read in `husserl-hua-14-…`), already concludes that "a priori such worlds … are not compossible with one another. There can be only one world, only one time, only one space with one nature" (Hua XIV 102). There the step to that conclusion runs through "a possible subject of a possible experience" of the plurality, who would have to be in empathy with each of its members (Hua XIV 101). In §60 that anonymous possible subject has become "me as the constituting primal monad for them", or a variation of me. The deduction is the same, but its centre has moved into the ego of the Meditations, and only with that move does Leibniz's name appear. The first version of the Fifth Meditation (spring 1929, *Zur Phänomenologie der Intersubjektivität III*, Text Nr. 1, read in `husserl-hua-15-…`) has no counterpart to §60. It passes from social communities (§11) directly to "the sense of phenomenological-transcendental 'idealism'" (§12, Hua XV 20), the paragraph that became the close of §62. The "metaphysical results", with their reckoning with Leibniz, belong to the revision. The understanding ends where §62 takes it up. Leibniz's metaphysics is kept as a logic of the possible and refused as a theology. The world is unique because I am given as a fact and can only vary myself and find the others, not because a creator chose it.

## §61, Hua I 173: a transcendental aesthetic without a transcendental analytic

Reads [§61, Paragraph 1](../commentaries/husserl/husserliana-1-cartesianische-meditationen-commentary.md#the-traditional-problems-of-psychological-origin-and-their-phenomenological-clarification).

Husserl takes one of Kant's titles and refuses the other. The research into the primordial world may be called "'transcendental aesthetic' in a very expanded sense", because Kant's arguments on space and time aim, however narrowly, at an a priori of what is intuited by the senses. But the storey above it is not to be called "transcendental analytic". For Kant, what lifts sensibility to objectivity is the understanding and its categories. For the Meditations, the first storey above the aesthetic is the theory of empathy, and objectivity is the foreignness of the other "carried over to the whole world". The pair of titles breaks where the other enters. That is the understanding the passage carries: the architecture of the *Critique* is kept for sensibility and replaced for objectivity, and the replacement is intersubjectivity.

> But if we return again to the transcendental attitude, then conversely our pre-delineations for the problematic of the psychological origin of the *representation of space*, etc., yield in turn pre-delineations for parallel transcendental-phenomenological problems, namely those of a concrete explication of primordial nature and world in general. This fills a great gap in the problematic of world-constitution as a transcendental phenomenon that we sketched earlier.
>
> We may also designate the extraordinarily large complex of researches relating to the primordinal world (which makes up a whole discipline) as "transcendental aesthetic" in a very expanded sense. We take over the *Kantian* title because the arguments on space and time in the critique of reason evidently aim, if in an extraordinarily restricted and unclarified way, at a noematic a priori of sensuous intuition. Expanded into the concrete a priori of purely sensuously intuitive nature (and indeed of the primordinal one), this a priori demands its phenomenological-transcendental completion by being drawn into a constitutive problematic. It would, however, not correspond to the sense of the Kantian counter-title "transcendental analytic" to use it now to name the higher storey of the constitutive a priori as well, that of the objective world itself and of the manifolds that constitute it (at the highest level, the *idealizing* and *theorizing* acts that finally constitute scientific nature and world). To the first storey rising above this "transcendental aesthetic" belongs the theory of the experience of others, of so-called *empathy*. It need only be pointed out that what we said of the psychological problems of origin of the lower storey holds here too: the problem of empathy first received its true sense and its true method of solution through constitutive phenomenology. Precisely for that reason all previous theories (including that of Max Scheler) have remained without real result, just as it has never been recognized how the foreignness of the *other* carries over to the whole world as its *objectivity*, first giving it this sense.

German:

> Kehren wir aber wieder in die transzendentale Einstellung zurück, so ergeben nun umgekehrt unsere Vorzeichnungen für die Problematik vom psychologischen Ursprung der *Raumvorstellung* usw. auch wieder Vorzeichnungen für transzendental-phänomenologische parallele Probleme, nämlich die einer konkreten Auslegung der primordialen Natur und Welt überhaupt — womit eine große Lücke in unserer früher entworfenen Problematik der Weltkonstitution als transzendentales Phänomen sich ausfüllt.
>
> Wir dürfen den außerordentlich großen Komplex der auf die primordinale Welt bezüglichen Forschungen (der eine ganze Disziplin ausmacht) auch bezeichnen als „transzendentale Ästhetik” in einem sehr erweiterten Sinn, wobei wir den *Kantischen* Titel darum übernehmen, weil die Raum- und Zeitargumente der Vernunftkritik offenbar, wenn auch in außerordentlich beschränkter und nicht abgeklärter Weise, auf ein noematisches Apriori sinnlicher Anschauung hinzielen, das, zum konkreten Apriori der rein sinnlich anschaulichen Natur (und zwar der primordinalen) erweitert, seine phänomenologisch-transzendentale Ergänzung fordert durch Einbeziehung in eine konstitutive Problematik. Allerdings würde es nicht dem Sinn des Kantischen Gegentitels „transzendentale Analytik” entsprechen, nun auch mit diesem das höhere Stockwerk des konstitutiven Apriori, der objektiven Welt selbst und das ihrer konstituierenden Mannigfaltigkeiten (in höchster Stufe der schließlich die wissenschaftliche Natur und Welt konstituierenden *idealisierenden* und *theoretisierenden* Akte) zu benennen. In das erste diese „transzendentale Ästhetik” übersteigende Stockwerk gehört die Theorie der Fremderfahrung, der sogenannten *Einfühlung*. Es bedarf nur des Hinweises darauf, daß hier dasselbe gilt, was wir für die psychologischen Ursprungsprobleme des unteren Stockwerkes gesagt haben, nämlich daß das Problem der Einfühlung erst durch die konstitutive Phänomenologie seinen wahren Sinn empfangen hat und seine wahre Methode der Lösung. Eben darum sind alle bisherigen Theorien (auch diejenige Max Schelers) ohne wirkliches Ergebnis geblieben, wie auch nie erkannt worden ist, wie sich die Fremdheit des *Anderen* auf die ganze Welt als ihre *Objektivität* überträgt, ihr diesen Sinn erst gebend.

*Note on the text.* The file closes both titles with a mismatched quotation mark („…”), quoted as it stands. It has both spellings, "primordial" and "primordinal", in one sentence; they are the edition's variants and nothing turns on them. The apparatus has no notes for Hua I 172–173.

The justification for taking over Kant's title is a causal clause with a hedge inside it, and the hedge carries as much as the clause. The title is taken "because" (*darum … weil*) the space and time arguments "evidently" (*offenbar*) aim at a "noematic a priori of sensuous intuition". The adjective is Husserl's, not Kant's. For Kant, space and time are forms of intuiting, belonging to the subject's receptivity. Husserl reads them as an a priori of what is intuited: the spatial and temporal structure of the sensuous thing as it appears. Read that way, the Transcendental Aesthetic already pointed toward what phenomenology describes, the synthesis of appearances of the "thing-phantom" in its "near-thing and far-thing" modifications that the preceding paragraphs had sketched (Hua I 172). The "evidently" claims that this was Kant's aim, and the concession "if in an extraordinarily restricted and unclarified way" admits that Kant did not see it as such. The expansion is double. The a priori of space and time is "expanded" (*erweitert*) into "the concrete a priori of purely sensuously intuitive nature", every structure of the sensuous thing and not its forms alone. And that a priori "demands" (*fordert*) its completion in a constitutive problematic, so that the a priori of the intuited is traced back to the syntheses that constitute it. "Very expanded" is therefore no courtesy. The title keeps from Kant only the level, that of sensibility, and the claim that this level has an a priori of its own.

The refusal of the counter-title is in the subjunctive and gives no reason: "It would, however, not correspond (*würde … nicht … entsprechen*) to the sense of the Kantian counter-title 'transcendental analytic'". The word "counter-title" (*Gegentitel*) names what is refused. In the *Critique* the analytic is the aesthetic's counterpart, and together they explain how an object of experience is possible: sensibility gives the manifold, and the understanding combines it into objects under categories. The higher storey Husserl means is "that of the objective world itself and of the manifolds that constitute it". Its highest level is that of the "*idealizing* and *theorizing* acts" that constitute "scientific nature". Kant's analytic would fit the top of this storey, the constitution of nature as the object of physics. What it would not fit is its first floor. The next sentence names the "first storey rising above this 'transcendental aesthetic'" (*das erste diese „transzendentale Ästhetik“ übersteigende Stockwerk*), and that is not the understanding. It is "the theory of the experience of others, of so-called *empathy*". The last clause gives what the subjunctive withheld. No one has recognized "how the foreignness of the *other* carries over to the whole world as its *objectivity*, first giving it this sense". In the Meditations, the step from a sensuous world to an objective world is taken by the other, not by the categories. A transcendental analytic in Kant's sense would skip that step. The reason for the refusal is here, but it has to be read out of the sentence's last clause. The sentence that refuses the title does not state it.

The wording around *empathy* keeps its distance from the tradition. "So-called" (*sogenannten*) is not new here. §43 had introduced the problem as a "*transcendental theory of the experience of others*, of so-called *empathy*" and had added that this theory "*co-founds a transcendental theory of the objective world*", "in particular also with regard to objective nature" (Hua I 124). The italic *Einfühlung* in §61 marks the received word, and "experience of others" (*Fremderfahrung*) remains Husserl's own. The charge against previous theories has a target: "including that of Max Scheler", the only time the Meditations name him. The ground of the charge is the parallel the paragraph has drawn. For the psychology of space, time and thing representation, "no physics and physiology" had anything to say, because these are "exclusively problems of intentional constitution" (Hua I 171). Empathy, too, gets "its true sense and its true method of solution" only from constitutive phenomenology. Scheler stands with the physiologists and psychologists of the lower storey. His theory is faulted for its method, not for its content.

The context makes the passage a map of the whole Fifth Meditation. §61 begins with the natural-scientific problems of genesis, the child who must build up its "*representation of the world*", and the generative problems of birth and death that "belong to a higher dimension" and are not yet working problems (Hua I 168–169). It then turns to what is a working problem, the origin of primordial nature, and sets out the psychological and transcendental versions in parallel (Hua I 169–172). The first paragraph of the passage states the gain of the parallel: "a great gap" in the earlier problematic of world-constitution is filled. §§44–47 had described the primordial world, but the clarifications since then were carried out on it as on "a ground pregiven to us" in which "we already found a world" (Hua I 169). The second paragraph names the levels. The aesthetic is the primordial nature of §§44–47. The first storey above it is the experience of others of §§49–54, and the higher levels are the objective nature of §55 and the communities and cultural worlds of §§56–58. The idealizing and theorizing acts at the top have no section of their own in the Meditations. The paragraph after the passage turns back to psychology and calls intentional psychology "the first in itself" of the sciences raised above naive positivity (Hua I 174).

Within the Meditations the Kantian vocabulary appears only twice. §41 had refused the name "Kantian idealism" for phenomenology, because Kant's idealism keeps open "at least as a limit-concept the possibility of a world of things in themselves", and it had refused as well an idealism "that wants to derive a meaningful world from meaningless sensual data" (Hua I 118; row 23). §61 takes from Kant what that refusal leaves: an a priori of sensibility, read noematically, with no unformed matter beneath it and no things in themselves behind it. The same scheme appears in the closing words of *Formal and Transcendental Logic*, and the contrast shows what the Meditations add. In an insertion from Husserl's own copy (*Handexemplar*), printed as a note to the *Schlußwort* (Hua XVII 297, read in `husserl-hua-17-…`, a machine-converted and unreviewed file whose sentence here reads cleanly), the "ground level" is "the 'transcendental aesthetic' in a new sense (so called by virtue of an easily grasped relation to the narrowly bounded Kantian one)" (*in einem neuen Sinne ,,transzendentale Ästhetik'' (so genannt vermöge einer leicht faßbaren Beziehung zu engumgrenzten Kantischen)*). It describes the a priori without which "unitary objects could not appear in mere experience and before the categorial actions". The main text goes on: "On this is now built the logos of objective worldly being and of science in the 'higher' sense", with its "exact" theories, geometry and Galilean physics. In the *Logic*, the step above the aesthetic is idealization. In the Meditations, idealization moves to the top of the higher storey and the other takes the first floor. The understanding ends with that change in order. Husserl keeps Kant's lower title because sensibility does have its own a priori, and he refuses the upper one because, in the Meditations, what makes the sensuous world objective is first another ego.

## §62, Hua I 175–177: the other mirrored in me, and not as original

Reads [§62, Paragraph 1](../commentaries/husserl/husserliana-1-cartesianische-meditationen-commentary.md#survey-characterization-of-the-intentional-interpretation-of-the-experience-of-others).

The answer to the charge of solipsism rests on a distinction between "in me" and "mine". The other constitutes himself in me, "*appresentatively* mirrored, and not as original". A mirror image is in the mirror, but what it shows is not part of the mirror. So the other's sense is formed in my life, and the other is not therefore a piece of my own being. The semblance that dissolves is the assumption that everything constituted in me must belong to me "as part of my own essence". That holds only of the immanent transcendences of my primordial sphere. Husserl calls the result a monadology "with all its deliberate echoes of Leibniz's metaphysics". The mirror is Leibniz's figure for the way a monad represents the universe. The qualifier "appresentatively" makes it Husserl's: the other is mirrored in me as what can never be presented, and that is why the mirroring does not make him mine.

> The objection dissolves in its groundlessness after the explications we have carried out. Above all it is to be noted that at no point has the transcendental attitude, that of the transcendental epochē, been abandoned, and that our *theory of the experience of others*, of the experience of *others*, wished to be and was permitted to be nothing more than the explication of its sense *other* from its constitutive accomplishment, and of the limit *truly existing others* [*sic*] from the corresponding syntheses of concordance.
>
> What I concordantly verify as an *other*, and so have given, by necessity and not by choice, as an actuality to be known, is in the transcendental attitude eo ipso the existing *other*, the *alter ego*, verified precisely within the experiencing intentionality of my *ego*. Within positivity we say, and find it self-evident, that in my own experience I experience not only myself but also, in the particular form of the experience of others, the *other*. The indubitable transcendental explication showed us not only the transcendental right of this positive statement. It showed that the transcendental *ego*, taken concretely (which in the transcendental reduction first becomes aware of itself with an undetermined horizon of itself), grasps both itself in its primordial own being and, in the form of its transcendental experience of others, *others*, other transcendental *egos*, although these are no longer given in originality and simple apodictic evidence, but in an evidence of *outer* experience. In me I experience, I know the *other*; in me he constitutes himself, *appresentatively* mirrored, and not as original. To that extent it can very well be said, in an *expanded* sense, that the *ego*, that I as the meditating explicator, gain all transcendence through *self-explication*, that is, explication of what I find in myself, and gain it as transcendentally constituted, not as accepted in naive positivity. So the semblance vanishes that everything that I, as *transcendental ego*, know from myself as existing and explicate as *constituted* in myself must belong to me myself as part of my own essence. That holds only of the *immanent transcendences*. Constitution, as the title for the systems of synthetic actuality and potentiality that confer sense and being on me as *ego* in my own essentiality, signifies constitution of immanent objective actuality. At the beginning of phenomenology, and in the attitude of one who is only beginning, who is just bringing the phenomenological reduction to primal instituting as the universal habitus of constitutive research, the transcendental ego that comes into view is indeed grasped apodictically, but with a quite undetermined horizon. That horizon is bound in generality only by this: the world and everything I know of it is to become mere *phenomenon*. So when I begin in this way, all the distinctions are missing that intentional explication first creates, and which nevertheless, as I see, belong to me essentially. Above all, the self-understanding about my primordial essence is missing, about my sphere of ownness in the pregnant sense, and about what constitutes itself in it, under the title experience of others, as foreign: as something appresented, but in principle not originally given in my primordial sphere itself, and never to be given there. I must first explicate my own as such in order to understand that within my own the not-own too receives being-sense, and indeed as something analogically appresented. So I, the meditator, do not understand at the beginning how I am to come at all to others and to myself, since other human beings are all *bracketed*. At bottom I also do not yet understand, and acknowledge only reluctantly, that I myself, *bracketing* myself as a human being and as a human person, am nevertheless to be preserved as ego. So I can as yet know nothing of a transcendental intersubjectivity. Involuntarily I take myself, the ego, for a *solus ipse*, and even after I have gained a first understanding of constitutive accomplishments I still take all constitutive contents for mere contents of my own, of this single ego. So the further explications of the present chapter were necessary. Only through them does the full and proper sense of phenomenological-transcendental "idealism" become intelligible to us. The semblance of a solipsism is dissolved, although the proposition retains its fundamental validity that everything that is for me can draw its being-sense exclusively from me myself, from my sphere of consciousness. This idealism proved to be a monadology which, with all its deliberate echoes of Leibniz's metaphysics, draws its content purely from the phenomenological explication of the transcendental experience laid bare by the transcendental reduction: from the most original evidence, in which all conceivable evidences must be grounded, or from the most original right, from which all rights, and in particular all rights of knowledge, can ever draw. Phenomenological explication is therefore really nothing like *metaphysical construction*, and not, either openly or covertly, a theorizing with assumptions or auxiliary thoughts taken over from the historical metaphysical tradition. It stands in the sharpest contrast to all that through its procedure within the frame of pure *intuition*, or rather of the pure explication of sense through fulfilling self-giving. In particular, with regard to the objective world of realities (as also to each of the manifold ideal objective worlds that are the fields of purely a priori sciences), it does nothing other (this cannot be impressed often enough) than *explicate the sense that this world has for all of us before any philosophizing*, and obviously has only from our experience: a sense that can be philosophically uncovered but never altered, and that, only from essential necessity and not from our weakness, carries with it in every actual experience horizons that need clarification in principle.

German:

> Der Einwand zerfließt in seiner Haltlosigkeit nach den durchgeführten Auslegungen. Es ist vor allem zu beachten, daß an keiner Stelle die transzendentale Einstellung, die der transzendentalen ἐποχή, verlassen worden ist und daß unsere *Theorie der Fremderfahrung*, der Erfahrung von *Anderen*, nichts weiteres sein wollte und sein durfte als die Auslegung ihres Sinnes *Anderer* aus ihrer konstitutiven Leistung und des Limes *wahrhaft seiender Anderers* aus den entsprechenden Synthesen der Einstimmigkeit.
>
> Was ich als *Anderen* einstimmig ausweise und dabei also in Notwendigkeit und nicht in Willkür als eine zu erkennende Wirklichkeit gegeben habe, das ist in transzendentaler Einstellung eo ipso der seiende *Andere*, das *alter ego*, ausgewiesen eben innerhalb der erfahrenden Intentionalität meines *ego*. Innerhalb der Positivität sagen wir und finden es selbstverständlich: in meiner eigenen Erfahrung erfahre ich nicht nur mich selbst, sondern in der besonderen Gestalt der Fremderfahrung den *Anderen*. Die zweifellose transzendentale Auslegung zeigte uns nicht nur das transzendentale Recht dieser positiven Aussage, sondern daß auch das transzendentale, konkret gefaßte *ego* (das in der transzendentalen Reduktion vorerst mit unbestimmtem Horizont seiner selbst inne wird) sowohl sich selbst in seinem primordialen Eigensein als auch, in Form seiner transzendentalen Fremderfahrung, *Andere*, andere transzendentale *ego* erfaßt, obschon sie nicht mehr in Originalität und schlichter apodiktischer Evidenz, sondern in einer Evidenz *äußerer* Erfahrung gegeben sind. In mir erfahre, erkenne ich den *Anderen*, in mir konstituiert er sich — *appräsentativ* gespiegelt, und nicht als Original. Insofern kann in einem *erweiterten* Sinne sehr wohl gesagt werden, daß das *ego*, daß ich als meditierender Auslegender, durch *Selbstauslegung*, nämlich Auslegung dessen, was ich in mir selbst finde, alle Transzendenz gewinne, und als transzendental konstituierte, also nicht als in naiver Positivität hingenommene. So verschwindet der Schein, daß alles, was ich als *transzendentales ego* aus mir selbst als seiend erkenne und als in mir selbst *Konstituiertes* auslege, mir selbst eigenwesentlich zugehören muß. Nur von den *immanenten Transzendenzen* gilt das; Konstitution als Titel für die mir als *ego* in der Eigenwesentlichkeit Sinn und Sein zueignenden Systeme synthetischer Aktualität und Potentialität besagt Konstitution von immanenter gegenständlicher Wirklichkeit. Zu Anfang der Phänomenologie und in der Einstellung des erst Anfangenden, der eben erst die phänomenologische Reduktion als universalen Habitus konstitutiven Forschens zur Urstiftung bringt, ist das in den Blick tretende transzendentale ego zwar apodiktisch erfaßt, aber mit einem ganz unbestimmten Horizont, der bloß dadurch in Allgemeinheit gebunden ist, daß die Welt und alles, was ich von ihr weiß, zu bloßem *Phänomen* werden soll. Es fehlen also, wenn ich so anfange, alle Unterscheidungen, die erst die intentionale Auslegung schafft, und die doch, wie ich einsehe, wesensmäßig zu mir gehören. Vor allem fehlt also die Selbstverständigung über mein primordiales Wesen, meine Eigenheitssphäre im prägnanten Sinne, und was in ihr selbst unter dem Titel Fremderfahrung als Fremdes, als ein appräsentiertes, aber prinzipiell nicht in meiner primordialen Sphäre selbst orginal Gegebenes und je zu Gebendes sich konstituiert. Ich muß erst das Eigene als solches auslegen, um zu verstehen, daß im Eigenen auch Nichteigenes Seinssinn bekommt, und zwar als analogisch Appräsentiertes. So verstehe ich, der Meditierende, am Anfang nicht, wie ich, da die anderen Menschen insgesamt *eingeklammert* sind, überhaupt zu Anderen und mir selbst kommen soll. Im Grunde verstehe ich auch noch nicht und erkenne es nur widerwillig an, daß ich selbst, mich als Menschen und als menschliche Person *einklammernd*, nun doch als ego erhalten bleiben soll. So kann ich noch nichts wissen von einer transzendentalen Intersubjektivität; unwillkürlich halte ich mich, das ego, für einen *solus ipse*, und halte alle konstitutiven Bestände, schon nachdem ich ein erstes Verständnis gewonnen habe für konstitutive Leistungen, immer noch für bloß eigene Gehalte dieses einzigen ego. So wären also die weitergehenden Auslegungen des vorliegenden Kapitels notwendige. Durch sie wird uns erst der volle und eigentliche Sinn des phänomenologisch-transzendentalen ,,Idealismus" verständlich. Der Schein eines Solipsismus ist aufgelöst, obschon der Satz die fundamentale Geltung behält, daß alles, was für mich ist, seinen Seinssinn ausschließlich aus mir selbst, aus meiner Bewußtseinssphäre schöpfen kann. Dieser Idealismus ergab sich als eine Monadologie, die bei allen absichtlichen Anklängen an Leibnizens Metaphysik ihren Gehalt rein aus der phänomenologischen Auslegung der durch die transzendentale Reduktion freigelegten transzendentalen Erfahrung schöpft, also aus der ursprünglichsten Evidenz, in der alle erdenklichen Evidenzen gründen müssen — oder aus dem ursprünglichsten Recht, aus dem alle Rechte und insbesondere Erkenntnisrechte je schöpfen können. Phänomenologische Auslegung ist also wirklich nichts dergleichen wie *metaphysische Konstruktion*, und nicht, weder offen noch versteckt, ein Theoretisieren mit übernommenen Voraussetzungen oder Hilfsgedanken aus der historischen metaphysischen Tradition. Sie steht zu all dem in schärfstem Gegensatz durch ihr Verfahren im Rahmen reiner *Intuition*, oder vielmehr der reinen Sinnauslegung durch erfüllende Selbstgebung. Insbesondere tut sie hinsichtlich der objektiven Welt der Realitäten (wie auch jeder der mannigfachen idealen objektiven Welten, die Felder rein apriorischer Wissenschaften sind) nichts anderes — das kann nicht oft genug eingeschärft werden — als den *Sinn auslegen, den diese Welt für uns alle vor jedem Philosophieren hat* und offenbar nur aus unserer Erfahrung hat, ein Sinn, der philosophisch enthüllt, aber nie geändert kann und der nur aus Wesensnotwendigkeit, und nicht aus unserer Schwäche, in jeder aktuellen Erfahrung Horizonte mit sich führt, die der prinzipiellen Klärung bedürfen.

*Note on the text.* The file has three slips in this passage, quoted as they stand: "wahrhaft seiender Anderers" (the genitive plural needs "Anderer"), "orginal" for *original*, and "nie geändert kann" [can never altered], which lacks *werden*. The first version (Hua XV 21) has "nie geändert werden kann", and the English follows that sense. The page markers are wrong here. The file sets the bare marker for Hua I 176 and the marker for 177 one after the other between the two paragraphs, as if p. 176 were lost. It is not. The apparatus records two manuscript readings on p. 176: at 176,20 the manuscript repeats "ich" before "überhaupt", and at 176,25 it has "ein" for "einen". Both fit the long paragraph: "wie ich, da die anderen Menschen insgesamt *eingeklammert* sind, überhaupt zu Anderen …" and "für einen *solus ipse*". The note at 177,31f. falls in the first paragraph of §63, after the file's marker for p. 178. Counting the print's lines from those notes, p. 175 runs from "Der Einwand zerfließt" to about "Zu Anfang der Phänomenologie", p. 176 from there to about the sentence on monadology, and p. 177 takes the rest of §62 and the opening of §63. These pages are inferred and may be out by a few lines. Nothing in §62 is missing.

The first paragraph sets the limit within which the answer must stay. The theory of the experience of others "wished to be and was permitted to be" (*sein wollte und sein durfte*) nothing more than an explication of sense. The two modal verbs say that the limit was chosen and also binding: a theory that left the epochē would have answered the objection by granting it. What the theory explicates is "its sense *other*" and "the limit *truly existing others*" (*des Limes wahrhaft seiender Anderer*). "Limit" (*Limes*) occurs nowhere else in the Meditations, but it names what §28 had said of every real object: it is "an infinite idea, related to infinities of experiences to be united concordantly", a "correlate-idea" of a complete synthesis of possible experiences (Hua I 97; row 18). The other's true being is a correlate of the same kind. So the second paragraph opens with an *eo ipso*. What I "concordantly verify" as other "is in the transcendental attitude eo ipso the existing *other*". The phrase "by necessity and not by choice" (*in Notwendigkeit und nicht in Willkür*) carries over §60's "I can only find them … I cannot create" (Hua I 168; row 28). The other is constituted in my experience, but I do not decide that he is there.

The central sentence is a chiasmus with "in me" at both ends. "In me I experience, I know the *other*; in me he constitutes himself" (*In mir erfahre, erkenne ich den Anderen, in mir konstituiert er sich*). The repetition grants the objection everything it can claim. The other is constituted in me, and no one else constitutes him for me. Then the dash and the qualification: "*appresentatively* mirrored, and not as original" (*appräsentativ gespiegelt, und nicht als Original*). The italic falls on the adverb, not on "mirrored". What Husserl stresses is the manner of the mirroring, not the fact. A mirror image is in the mirror without being part of it, and the image is not the original it shows. The sentence before has said how the other is given: "no longer in originality and simple apodictic evidence, but in an evidence of *outer* experience". The other is in me in the way the outer world is: given, and given as not me. §54 had made the same point from the other side. Appresentation "never demands or admits fulfilment through presentation" (Hua I 148; row 27). "Not as original" is that impossibility stated as a manner of being in me.

The next three sentences turn the distinction into a correction of the objection's premise. "In an *expanded* sense" (*in einem erweiterten Sinne*) I gain "all transcendence through *self-explication*". The italic on "expanded" concedes that the formula is loose: self-explication now includes explicating what I find in myself as not mine. Then: "So the semblance vanishes" (*So verschwindet der Schein*) that everything constituted in me "must belong to me myself as part of my own essence" (*mir selbst eigenwesentlich zugehören muß*). This is the semblance the objection lives on. The rule holds only of "the *immanent transcendences*", the things of the primordial world, which §61 had called "a *world* of immanent transcendences" (Hua I 169). For them, constitution "signifies constitution of immanent objective actuality". For the other it does not. Constitution in me is one thing and belonging to me is another, and the objection had taken them as one.

The paragraph then explains why the semblance arises at all, and the explanation is a story about the beginner. The one who is "just bringing the phenomenological reduction to primal instituting" (*zur Urstiftung bringt*) has an ego grasped apodictically "but with a quite undetermined horizon". The distinctions that explication "first creates" are missing, above all that between my own and what is "appresented, but in principle not originally given". So "involuntarily (*unwillkürlich*) I take myself, the ego, for a *solus ipse*". Solipsism is not a thesis anyone holds. It is the beginner's involuntary misunderstanding of his own starting point, and it lasts as long as the reduction has been instituted but not yet explicated. §42 had asked whether, reduced to my transcendental ego, I have not "become *solus ipse*" (*zum solus ipse geworden*, Hua I 121; row 24). §62 answers that the meditator had only taken himself for one. The term from §50, primal instituting, is now applied to the reduction itself (row 26). The reduction, like every apperception, has a first time, and its sense is not finished at that time.

The close of the paragraph names the result and answers the objection's last charge. The objection had said that the step to the other was possible only through "an unacknowledged metaphysics, through a secret takeover of Leibnizian traditions" (*durch eine uneingestandene Metaphysik, durch eine geheime Übernahme Leibnizischer Traditionen*, Hua I 174). The answer keeps Leibniz and changes the adjective: a monadology "with all its deliberate echoes of Leibniz's metaphysics" (*bei allen absichtlichen Anklängen an Leibnizens Metaphysik*). What was "secret" and "unacknowledged" becomes "deliberate". The echoes are admitted and intended, and what is denied is that they supply the content. That content is drawn "purely" from transcendental experience. The verb tense marks the change from the first version. In the spring 1929 draft (Text Nr. 1, §12, Hua XV 20, read in `husserl-hua-15-…`), the idealism "turns out to be" (*ergibt sich als*) a monadology. In §62 it "proved to be" (*ergab sich als*) one, a result reached by the chapter. The draft says that the semblance of solipsism "vanishes" (*verschwindet*). §62 says that it "is dissolved" (*ist aufgelöst*) and keeps "vanishes" for the earlier semblance, that everything constituted in me is mine. The draft contains only this closing part. The account of the beginner and the distinction between in me and mine were added in the revision. So was the insistence: "really (*wirklich*) nothing like *metaphysical construction*", and "this cannot be impressed often enough". At one word the two files differ in sense. The draft calls the world's pre-philosophical sense one that "can be philosophically distorted, but never altered" (*philosophisch entstellt, aber nie geändert werden kann*). The Hua I file has "uncovered" (*enthüllt*). Neither file is the manuscript, and the apparatus of Hua I has no note here, so the difference cannot be settled from the corpus.

The mirror has a longer history in the work and outside it. §44 introduced the other as an ego "*mirroring* itself in my own I, my monad", and then corrected the figure at once: "the other is a mirroring of myself, and yet not properly a mirroring; an analogue of myself, and yet again not an analogue in the usual sense" (Hua I 125). The first version has almost the same sentence (Hua XV 7). In its §9, the draft had first said that the other egos are in mine "mirrored" (*gespiegelt*). Husserl replaced the word with "experienced in the most original manner through presentifying 'manifestation'" (*in ursprünglichster Weise erfahren durch vergegenwärtigende „Bekundung“*); the apparatus records the change (Hua XV 17,1–2). In its §11 the same draft speaks of the monads as interwoven "through 'mirroring', which is no unsubstantial semblance" (*durch „Spiegelung“, die kein wesenloser Schein ist*, Hua XV 20). Husserl hesitated over the word in 1929, and §62 keeps it with a qualifier. The word is Leibniz's, and Husserl knew its range. A note of about 1922, "Mirrorings (= representations) in Leibniz" (*Zur Phänomenologie der Intersubjektivität II*, Beilage XL, Hua XIV 298–299, read in `husserl-hua-14-…`), separates five senses of the word. Among them, each monad "'mirrors' (represents) the 'universe'", and "the absolute universe of monads" mirrors itself in each monad. In the note's fourth sense, every monad mirrors the universe of monads. In §62 the other is mirrored in me "*appresentatively*", and appresentation is by its sense what can never become presentation. Leibniz's representation tells us what each monad contains. Husserl's appresentation also says what my monad cannot contain. That is where the understanding ends. Being constituted in me is the other's way of being for me, and the mirroring that gives him to me also keeps him from being part of me.
